Higher-Order Behavioural Conformances via Fibrations Journal: PACMPL Volume: 1 CONF 1 1 CCS: Theory of computation Categorical semantics CCS: Theory of computation Operational semantics
Henning Urbat

Abstract
. Coinduction is a widely used technique for establishing behavioural equivalence of programs in higher-order languages. In recent years, the rise of languages with quantitative (e.g. probabilistic) features has led to extensions of coinductive methods to more refined types of behavioural conformances, most notably notions of behavioural distance. To guarantee soundness of coinductive reasoning, one needs to show that the behavioural conformance at hand forms a program congruence, i.e. it is suitably compatible with the operations of the language. This is usually achieved by a complex proof technique known as Howe’s method , which needs to be carefully adapted to both the specific language and the targeted notion of behavioural conformance. We develop a uniform categorical approach to Howe’s method that features two orthogonal dimensions of abstraction: (1) the underlying higher-order language is modelled by an abstract higher-order specification ( AHOS ), a novel and very general categorical account of operational semantics, and (2) notions of behavioural conformance (such as relations or metrics) are modelled via fibrations over the base category of an AHOS. Our main result is a fundamental congruence theorem at this level of generality: Under natural conditions on the categorical ingredients and the operational rules of a language modelled by an AHOS, the greatest behavioural (bi)conformance on its operational model forms a congruence. We illustrate our theory by deriving congruence of bisimilarity and behavioural pseudometrics for probabilistic higher-order languages.



1

1.
Introduction

One of the most fundamental, and most challenging, questions in the theory of higher-order languages concerns the
behavioural equivalence
of programs: what precisely does it mean for two programs to ‘behave in the same way’, and how does one actually prove it? In the world of operational semantics, the ubiquitous notion of program equivalence is
contextual equivalence

(
Morris 1968
)
. Two programs
p
and
q
are contextually equivalent if, whichever context (program with a hole)
C[\cdot]
they are plugged into, the resulting programs
C[p]
and
C[q]
expose the same observable behaviour, e.g. they co-terminate, terminate with the same probability, or output the same values.

While the definition of contextual equivalence is simple and natural, it is somewhat difficult to work with: to prove that
p
and
q
are contextually equivalent, one would need to analyse the observable behaviour of
C[p]
and
C[q]
for all (infinitely many) contexts at the same time. Therefore, efficient proof techniques are needed. One fundamental approach is given by
coinduction
. Here, the idea is to view the set of programs as an
applicative transition system

(
Abramsky 1990
)
, that is, a labelled transition system (LTS) whose states and labels are both given by programs. For instance, in the case of the untyped call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus, the term
\lambda x.t
has the outgoing transitions
\lambda x.t\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$e$}}\;}}$}}t[e/x]
for all
\lambda
-terms
e
, reflecting the intuition that
\lambda x.t
behaves like a function mapping the input
e
to the output
t[e/x]
. Like every LTS, the applicative transition system on
\lambda
-terms comes with a notion of
bisimilarity

(
Sangiorgi 2011
)
expressing behavioural equivalence of states. The key observation, due to
Abramsky 1990
, is that bisimilarity implies contextual equivalence. Thus, coinduction yields a sound proof technique: to show that
p
and
q
are contextually equivalent, it suffices to find a bisimulation containing them. Usually, this is much easier than proving contextual equivalence directly.

The soundness of coinduction essentially amounts to the fact that the bisimilarity relation is a
congruence
, that is, compatible with the operations of the language. The standard approach to establishing that congruence property is
Howe’s method

(
Howe 1989
;
Howe 1996
)
. It has proven extremely robust and has been adapted to derive soundness results for a wide range of languages, including languages with different forms of evaluation (call-by-name
(
Pitts 2011
)
, call-by-value
(
Pitts 2011
)
, call-by-push-value
(
Goncharov et al. 2025c
)
), typed languages
(
Pitts 1997
)
, probabilistic languages (both discrete
(
Lago et al. 2014
)
and continuous
(
Lago and Gavazzo 2019
)
), stateful languages
(
Jeffrey and Rathke 1999
;
Goncharov et al. 2025a
)
, languages with monadic computational effects
(
Lago et al. 2017
)
, higher-order process calculi
(
Lenglet and Schmitt 2015
)
, and many more. Additionally, the emergence of quantitative (e.g. probabilistic) higher-order languages has led to quantitative extensions of Howe’s method, where contextual equivalence is replaced with the more fine-grained notion of
contextual distance
(measuring how much contexts can discriminate programs) and bisimilarity is generalized to
behavioural pseudometrics

(
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
;
Gavazzo 2018
)
.

In practice, the use of Howe’s method is highly non-trivial. This is mainly for three reasons:

(1)

Howe’s method is typically applied on a per case basis, that is, its principles are reinvented from scratch for every individual language and carefully adapted to its features. Every modification of the language at hand (e.g. extending its type system, adding states, adding probabilistic features) essentially requires starting anew, or at least laboriously reworking existing proofs.

(2)

Orthogonally, there is a wide spectrum of
behavioural conformances

(
Beohar et al. 2024
)
of interest. Is the targeted notion plain behavioural equivalence, or some kind of behavioural distance? In the latter case, are distances supposed to be rational numbers, or real numbers, or more complex objects, such as functions
(
Dal Lago et al. 2019
)
, or elements of a quantale
(
Gavazzo 2018
)
? Does ‘congruence’ mean non-expansivity of the language operations w.r.t. some distance, or something else, like Lipschitz-continuity? The suitable type of conformance depends on the nature of the given language and the kind of reasoning tasks one aims to address, and again every choice calls for its own incarnation of Howe’s method.

(3)

Even for a fixed language and conformance type, congruence proofs based on Howe’s method tend to be complex and subtle, requiring tedious case distinctions over the syntax and operational rules of the language, while at the same containing plenty of ‘bookkeeping’ tasks that essentially work in the same way for all languages and bloat the argument. The high-level intuition
why
the method works is not always easy to grasp. This difficulty can be partly attributed to the fact that coinduction is an inherently first-order concept that is now applied to a higher-order setting.

Contribution.

We present a novel
categorical
approach to coinductive reasoning on higher-order languages that addresses all three issues discussed above at the same time.

(1)

One key reason for the need to reinvent Howe’s method for every language is that there is simply no sufficiently universal (categorical) notion of
higher-order language
. We introduce
abstract higher-order specifications
(
\emph{AHOS}
), a general and flexible categorical formalism that presents the operational semantics of a higher-order language as a
rule morphism
in a category
\mathbb{B}
. Roughly, a rule morphism specifies the operational rules of a language by distributing its syntax (given by an endofunctor
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
) over its behaviour (given by a mixed variance bifunctor
B\colon\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
). Every AHOS naturally induces an
operational model
, a transition system (higher-order coalgebra)
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
on the object
\Lambda
of program terms that runs programs according to the given rules. A related idea appears in the
higher-order abstract GSOS
framework recently introduced by
Goncharov et al. 2023
. The latter captures operational rules whose model is definable via
structural induction
, typical for small-step operational semantics. However, it is not capable of handling the more powerful
rule induction
needed for big-step semantics, which is, e.g., the standard semantics for coinductive reasoning on probabilistic languages. AHOS overcome this limitation by combining the principles of higher-order abstract GSOS with Rot’s
(first-order) monotone biGSOS

(
Rot 2019
)
framework.

(2)

To get a handle on the plethora of different types of behavioural conformance, we put a
fibration
above the base category of an AHOS. Fibrations allow to model notions of behavioural equivalence and behavioural distance for coalgebras in a flexible and uniform way
(
Hermida and Jacobs 1998
;
Baldan et al. 2018
)
. In fact, in the fibrational framework, moving from qualitative to quantitative reasoning just amounts to switching from a fibration of relations to a fibration of suitable fuzzy relations.

(3)

We develop Howe’s method at the generality of higher-order languages modelled by AHOS and fibrational behavioural conformances. This enables us to establish a strikingly general congruence result (
Theorem

6.7
), which informally states:

If the operational rules specifying a higher-order language are sufficiently monotone and continuous, then the greatest behavioural (bi)conformance on its operational model forms a congruence.

Here the
greatest behavioural (bi)conformance
is the fibrational abstraction of (bi)similarity and its quantitative analogues. The importance of this abstract result is that it systematically reduces the congruence property of a language to a number of simple conditions on the categorical ingredients and the operational rules of the language, while suppressing the ‘generic’, language-independent aspects of ad hoc congruence proofs. In this way, the complexity of deriving congruence results is dramatically reduced. We will demonstrate this point by giving short and simple congruence proofs for probabilistic higher-order languages corresponding to probabilistic
\lambda
-calculi
(
Lago et al. 2014
;
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
)
.

Related Work

The need for categorical tools to address the complexities of Howe’s method has been identified by several researchers, and systematic categorical accounts of Howe’s method have been an active topic of investigation in recent years. However, unlike our present paper, all approaches so far were limited to reasoning about behavioural
equivalence
, rather than quantitative notions of behavioural distance or (fibrational) conformances in general. Moreover, they model languages in categorical frameworks that are more specific than our very general AHOS setup.

Urbat et al. 2023
develop Howe’s method at the generality of the higher-order abstract GSOS framework
(
Goncharov et al. 2023
)
, which models languages with a small-step operational semantics by suitable dinatural transformations. They prove a congruence result for the similarity relation on operational models. The non-trivial step to
bi
similarity is missing. In the present paper, we achieve this step by developing Howe’s
transitive closure trick
at fibrational generality.

Borthelle et al. 2020
and
Hirschowitz and Lafont 2022
model operational rules
as endofunctors on a specific presheaf category of
transition systems
over models
of a signature endofunctor, and the initial algebra for the rule
endofunctor represents the induced transition system for the given
semantics. In this setting, they achieve a congruence result for bisimilarity. Their categorical setup is substantially different, and technically more complex, than AHOS.

Dal Lago et al.
(
Dal Lago et al. 2017
)
develop Howe’s method and prove a congruence result for bisimilarity in a setting of call-by-value
\lambda
-calculi
with algebraic effects, based on the theory of relators. Their notion
of a
computational

\lambda
-calculus is parametrized over a
signature
\Sigma
and a monad
T
on the category of sets, representing syntax and
effects of the language.

While fibrations have not appeared in the literature on coinduction for higher-order languages, their general usefulness for operational reasoning has been recognized. For instance, in recent work,
Dagnino and Gavazzo 2024
model notions of operational logical relations in a fibrational setting.

2.
Preliminaries

Our abstract approach to behavioural conformances for higher-order languages uses some basic concepts from category theory
(
Mac Lane 1978
)
, in particular, algebras for a functor (which capture sets of program terms) and coalgebras for a functor (which capture applicative transition systems). In the following we review some categorical terminology used throughout our paper.

Notation

For objects

X_{1},X_{2}
of a category
{\mathbb{C}}
, we denote their product by
X_{1}\times X_{2}
, the projections by
{\mathsf{outl}}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{1}
and
{\mathsf{outr}}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{2}
, and
the pairing of morphisms
f_{i}\colon X\to X_{i}
,
i=1,2
, by
\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\colon X\to X_{1}\times X_{2}
. We write

X_{1}+X_{2}
for the coproduct,
{\mathsf{inl}}\colon X_{1}\to X_{1}+X_{2}
and

{\mathsf{inr}}\colon X_{2}\to X_{1}+X_{2}
for its injections,
[g_{1},g_{2}]\colon X_{1}+X_{2}\to X
for the copairing of morphisms
g_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X
,

i=1,2
, and
\nabla=[{\mathsf{id}}_{X},{\mathsf{id}}_{X}]\colon X+X\to X
for the codiagonal. For every category
{\mathbb{C}}
, we let
{\mathbb{C}}^{(2)}
denote the category that has the same objects as
{\mathbb{C}}
, and as morphisms
(f,g)\colon X\to Y
all pairs of morphisms from
X
to
Y
in
{\mathbb{C}}
. Composition and identities are formed componentwise.
Note that
(\mathbb{B}\times{\mathbb{C}})^{(2)}\cong\mathbb{B}^{(2)}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{(2)}
and
({\mathbb{C}}^{\mathsf{op}})^{(2)}\cong({\mathbb{C}}^{(2)})^{\mathsf{op}}
. Every functor
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to{\mathbb{C}}
induces a functor
F^{(2)}\colon\mathbb{B}^{(2)}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{(2)}
given by

F^{(2)}X=FX
and
F^{(2)}(f,g)=(Ff,Fg)
. The category
{\mathbb{C}}
forms a non-full subcategory of
{\mathbb{C}}^{(2)}
via the embedding functor

I\colon{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrowtail{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
given by
X\mapsto X
and
f\mapsto(f,f)
.

Algebras

Let
\Sigma
be an endofunctor on a category
\mathbb{C}
. A
\Sigma
-algebra

(A,a)
consists of an object
A
(the
carrier
of the
algebra) and a morphism
a\colon\Sigma A\to A
(its
structure
). A

morphism
from
(A,a)
to a
\Sigma
-algebra
(B,b)
is a morphism

h\colon A\to B
of
\mathbb{C}
such that
h\circ a=b\circ\Sigma h
. Algebras
for
\Sigma
and their morphisms form a category, which we denote by
\Alg(\Sigma)
. An
initial algebra
is an initial object of
\Alg(\Sigma)
; if it exists, we denote its carrier by
\mu\Sigma

and its structure by
{\iota\colon\Sigma(\mu\Sigma)\to\mu\Sigma}
. The structure
\iota
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbb{C}}
.
A
free
\Sigma
-algebra
generated by an object
X
of
\mathbb{C}
is a

\Sigma
-algebra
(\Sigma^{\star}X,\iota_{X})
together with a morphism

\eta_{X}\colon X\to\Sigma^{\star}X
of
\mathbb{C}
such that for every algebra
(A,a)

and every morphism
h\colon X\to A
in
\mathbb{C}
, there exists a unique

\Sigma
-algebra morphism
h^{\#}\colon(\Sigma^{\star}X,\iota_{X})\to(A,a)

such that
h=h^{\#}\circ\eta_{X}
; the morphism
h^{\#}
is called the
free
extension
of
h
. If a free
\Sigma
-algebra exists on every object
X\in{\mathbb{C}}
, then their formation extends to a monad
\Sigma^{\star}\colon{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the
free monad
generated by
\Sigma
. For every
\Sigma
-algebra
(A,a)
we write

\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}\colon\Sigma^{\star}A\to A
for the free extension of
{\mathsf{id}}_{A}\colon A\to A
.

A prime example of functor algebras are algebras for a
signature. An
(algebraic) signature
consists of a set
\Sigma

of
operation symbols
and a map
\mathsf{ar}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{N}

associating to every
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
its
arity
. Operation symbols of arity
0
are called
constants
. Every
signature
\Sigma
induces an endofunctor on the category
\mathbf{Set}
of sets and functions, denoted by the
same letter
\Sigma
and defined by
\Sigma X=\coprod_{{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma}X^{\mathsf{ar}({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}})}
. (Such endofunctors are called
polynomial functors
.) An algebra for the functor
\Sigma
is
precisely an algebra for the signature
\Sigma
: a set
A
with an operation
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}\colon A^{n}\to A
for every
n
-ary operation symbol
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
. Morphisms of
\Sigma
-algebras are
maps respecting the algebraic structure. Given a set
X
of
variables, the free algebra
\Sigma^{\star}X
is the
\Sigma
-algebra of

\Sigma
-terms with variables from
X
; more precisely,
\Sigma^{\star}X
is inductively defined by
X\subseteq\Sigma^{\star}X
and
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Sigma^{\star}X
for all
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
of arity
n
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\Sigma^{\star}X
.
The free
algebra on the empty set is the initial algebra
\mu\Sigma
; it is
formed by all
closed terms
of the signature. For every

\Sigma
-algebra
(A,a)
, the induced map

\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle a$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}$}}\colon\Sigma^{\star}A\to A
evaluates terms in
A
.

Coalgebras

Dually to the notion of algebra, a
coalgebra
for an endofunctor
B
on
{\mathbb{C}}
is a pair
(C,c)
consisting of an object
C
(the

state space
) and a morphism
c\colon C\to BC
(its

structure
). A
cofree
B
-coalgebra
generated by an object
X
of
\mathbb{C}
is a

B
-coalgebra
(B^{\infty}X,\tau_{X})
together with a morphism

\varepsilon_{X}\colon B^{\infty}X\to X
of
\mathbb{C}
such that for every coalgebra
(C,c)

and every morphism
h\colon C\to X
in
\mathbb{C}
, there exists a unique

B
-coalgebra morphism
\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}\colon(C,c)\to(B^{\infty}X,\tau_{X})

such that
h=\varepsilon_{X}\circ{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}$}}}
; the morphism

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}

is called the
cofree
extension
of
h
. (We use the same notation
\widehat{(-)}
for free and cofree extensions for symmetry; the kind of extension will always be clear from the context.)
If a cofree coalgebra exists on every
X\in{\mathbb{C}}
, their formation yields a comonad
B^{\infty}\colon{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the
cofree comonad
generated by
B
.

Informally, coalgebras are abstractions of transition systems, and
B^{\infty}X
consists of all possible unravelled behaviours of
B
-coalgebras whose states are colored using colors from
X
. For example, for the polynomial functor
BC=C\times C+1
on
\mathbf{Set}
, a coalgebra
c\colon C\to C\times C+1
corresponds to a deterministic labelled transition system with labels
\{l,r\}
where every state either has a single outgoing
l
-transition and a single outgoing
r
-transition, or is terminating. The cofree coalgebra
B^{\infty}X
is given by the set of all (possibly infinite) ordered binary trees with
X
-colored nodes.

A
higher-order coalgebra
for a mixed variance bifunctor
B\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a pair
(C,c)
of an object
C
and a morphism
c\colon C\to B(C,C)
, that is, a coalgebra for the endofunctor
B(C,-)
with state space
C
. Higher-order coalgebras can be regarded as an abstract capturing of
applicative transition systems

(
Abramsky 1990
)
, i.e. labelled transition systems where both the states and the transition labels are given by programs of some higher-order language; see the next section for examples. Bisimulations and behavioural metrics of higher-order programs are studied in terms of such transition systems.

Probability Distributions

A
countable probability distribution
on a set
X
is a map
\varphi\colon X\to[0,1]
whose support
\{x\in X\mid\varphi(x)\neq 0\}
is a countable set and that satisfies
\sum_{x\in X}\varphi(x)=1
. We represent a distribution
\varphi
as a formal sum
\varphi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i}
where
I
is a countable index set,
x_{i}\in X
for all
i\in I
, and
\varphi(x)=\sum_{x_{i}=x}p_{i}
for all
x\in X
. For
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}X
and
p\in[0,1]
we write
p\cdot\varphi+(1-p)\cdot\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}X
for the distribution
x\mapsto p\cdot\varphi(x)+(1-p)\cdot\psi(x)
. The
(countable) distribution functor

{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
associates to each set
X
the set
{\mathcal{D}}X
of countable probability distributions on
X
, and to each map
f\colon X\to Y
the map
{\mathcal{D}}f\colon{\mathcal{D}}X\to{\mathcal{D}}X
given by
{\mathcal{D}}f(\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
. The functor
{\mathcal{D}}
extends to a monad with unit
\eta\colon X\to{\mathcal{D}}X
and multiplication
\mu\colon{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{D}}X\to{\mathcal{D}}X
given by

\eta(x)=1\cdot x\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot\varphi_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i}\cdot p_{i,j}\cdot x_{i,j}\quad\text{where}\quad\varphi_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i,j}\cdot x_{i,j}.

Finally, we let
\mathsf{st}\colon{\mathcal{D}}X\times Y\to{\mathcal{D}}(X\times Y)
denote the (right) strength of the monad
{\mathcal{D}}
, given by

\mathsf{st}(\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i},y)=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot(x_{i},y).

3.
Probabilistic Combinatory Logic

To illustrate the developments of this paper, we introduce as running examples two simple probabilistic higher-order languages that take the form of combinatory logics
(
Hindley and Seldin 2008
)
. Recall that combinatory logics serve as alternative presentations of
\lambda
-calculi that avoid explicit notions of variables, binding,
\alpha
-renaming, and substitution. This technical simplification allows us to model the syntax and operational semantics of our languages within the category of sets and focus on the key aspects of our work. More complex higher-order languages, featuring e.g. variables, types, store, can be modelled by moving froms sets to suitable presheaf categories (cf.
Section

8
for a discussion).

Our two example languages,
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, are probabilistic extensions of the well-known combinatory logics
\mathbf{SKI}
and
\mathbf{BCKI}

(
Hindley and Seldin 2008
)
, respectively.
\mathbf{SKI}
is computationally equivalent to the untyped call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus, while
\mathbf{BCKI}
is computationally equivalent to the
affine
untyped call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus. The latter is the restriction of the
\lambda
-calculus to
affine

\lambda
-terms, where in no subterm a variable is allowed to occur freely more than once. For instance,
\lambda x.\lambda y.x\,y
is an affine
\lambda
-term, but
\lambda x.\,x\,x
is not, because the subterm
x\,x
has two free occurrences of
x
.

3.1.
\mathbf{pSKI}

The terms of
\mathbf{pSKI}
are formed over the signature

(3.1)

\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}=\{\,S:0,\,S^{\prime}:1,\,S^{\prime\prime}:2,\,K:0,\,K^{\prime}:1,\,I:0,\,\Omega:0,\,\mathsf{app}:2,\,\oplus:2\,\},

with arities as indicated. We let
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
denote the set of closed
\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
-terms (i.e.
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}=\mu\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
). The intended meaning of the above operations is as follows. The binary operator
\mathsf{app}
corresponds to function application; accordingly, we write
t\,s
for
\mathsf{app}(t,s)
.
The constant
\Omega
represents a non-terminating computation. The binary operator
\oplus
is probabilistic choice: the term
t\oplus s
behaves like
t
or
s
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
each. The constants
S
,
K
,
I
represent three specific
\lambda
-terms, namely

S=\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,(x\,z)\,(y\,z),\qquad K=\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x,\qquad I=\lambda x.\,x.

Finally, the primed versions of
S
and
K
correspond to applications of the respective
\lambda
-terms: the terms
S^{\prime}(t)
,
S^{\prime\prime}(t,s)
, and
K^{\prime}(t)
behave like
S\,t
,
S\,t\,s
, and
K\,t
, respectively. Our presentation of combinatory logics using the auxiliary primed operators follows
Di Gianantonio et al. 2008
and leads to a consistent operational semantics where all combinators accept exactly one argument, which is well-suited for reasoning about behavioural conformances. In particular, one cannot tell combinators apart just based on the number of their arguments.

The (big-step) operational semantics of
\mathbf{pSKI}
consists of judgements of the form

t\Downarrow\varphi\qquad\text{where}\qquad\text{$t\in{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$\varphi\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}^{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}})$},

specified by the rules of
Figure

1
with
s,t,u
ranging over
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
.

\displaystyle~S\Downarrow 1\cdot(t\mapsto S^{\prime}(t))~~~\qquad~S^{\prime}(t)\Downarrow 1\cdot(s\mapsto S^{\prime\prime}(t,s))~~~\qquad~S^{\prime\prime}(t,s)\Downarrow 1\cdot(u\mapsto(t\,u)\,(s\,u))~~~

\displaystyle~K\Downarrow 1\cdot(t\mapsto K^{\prime}(t))~~~\qquad~K^{\prime}(t)\Downarrow 1\cdot(s\mapsto t)~~~\qquad~I\Downarrow 1\cdot(t\mapsto t)~~~

\displaystyle~\Omega\Downarrow 1\cdot\bot~~~\qquad~t\oplus s\Downarrow\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\psi~~t\Downarrow\varphi\quad s\Downarrow\psi~\qquad~t\,s\Downarrow p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i}\cdot p_{i,j}\cdot b_{i,j}~~t\Downarrow p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot f_{i}\quad f_{i}(s)\Downarrow\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i,j}\cdot b_{i,j}\,(i\in I)~

Figure 1
.
Operational semantics of
\mathbf{pSKI}
.

Informally,
t\Downarrow p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot f_{i}
means that the program
t
diverges with probability
p
, and computes the function
f_{i}\in{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}^{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}
with probability
p_{i}
. To make this precise, we consider the behaviour bifunctor

(3.2)

B\colon\mathbf{Set}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}\qquad\text{given by}\qquad B(X,Y)={\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+Y^{X}).

The set
B({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}})
carries a natural DCPO structure given by the order
\varphi\leq\psi
iff
\varphi(f)\leq\psi(f)
for all
f\in{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}^{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}
. By extension, the set
\mathbf{Set}({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},B({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}))
of higher-order coalgebras on
{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}
is also a DCPO with the pointwise partial order
\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}
iff
\gamma(t)\leq\gamma^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}
. Given a higher-order coalgebra
\gamma\colon\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}\to B(\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}},\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}})
we write
t\Downarrow\varphi
if
\gamma(t)=\varphi
, and we say that
\gamma

satisfies
the rules of
Figure

1
if whenever there are
\gamma
-transitions matching the premises of some rule, then there is a
\gamma
-transition given by its conclusion.
The
operational model
of
\mathbf{pSKI}
is the
least
higher-order coalgebra

(3.3)

\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}\colon\Lambda_{p\mathbf{SKI}}\to B(\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}},\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}})

satisfying the rules of
Figure

1
. This corresponds to the standard approximation semantics for probabilistic
\lambda
-calculi
(
Lago and Zorzi 2012
)
. Note that the least coalgebra
\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
exists by the Knaster-Tarski theorem
1
1

1

Every monotone endomap on a DCPO with
\bot
has a least fixed point.
: it is the least fixed point of the monotone map

(3.4)

\Gamma\colon\mathbf{Set}({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},B({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}))\to\mathbf{Set}({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},B({\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}},{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}))

that sends a coalgebra
\gamma
to the coalgebra
\Gamma(\gamma)
induced by the rules of
Figure

1
; for instance,
\Gamma(\gamma)(S)=1\cdot(t\mapsto S^{\prime}(t))
and if
\gamma(t)=\varphi
and
\gamma(s)=\psi
, then
\Gamma(\gamma)(t\oplus s)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\psi
. Monotonicity of
\Gamma
amounts to the observation that the rules of
Figure

1
are monotone: whenever the premises of a rule are grown according to the order
\leq
, then the conclusion also grows. For instance, in the case of the rule for
\oplus
, if
\varphi\leq\varphi^{\prime}
and
\psi\leq\psi^{\prime}
, then
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\psi\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\psi^{\prime}
.

A natural notion of program equivalence between
\mathbf{pSKI}
-terms is given by
contextual equivalence

(
Lago et al. 2014
)
. A
context
is a
\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
-term in the single variable ‘
\cdot
’ (the ‘hole’), which appears at most once in the term. We denote a context by
C[\cdot]
and write
C[t]
for the outcome of substituting a term
t\in\Lambda
for the hole.

Contextual equivalence
is the equivalence relation on
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
defined by

t\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}_{\mathrm{ctx}}s\iff\forall C[\cdot].\,\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}(C[t])(\bot)=\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}(C[s])(\bot).

Thus, two programs are equivalent if in every context they terminate with the same probability. Our theory of congruence developed below will provide coinductive methods to reason about
\approx_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pSKI}}
.

3.2.
\mathbf{pBCKI}

The terms of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
are formed over the signature

(3.5)

\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}=\{\,B:0,\,B^{\prime}:1,\,B^{\prime\prime}:2,\,C:0,\,C^{\prime}:1,\,C^{\prime\prime}:2,\,K:0,\,K^{\prime}:1,\,I:0,\,\Omega:0,\,\mathsf{app}:2,\,\oplus:2\,\}.

We let
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
denote the set of closed
\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
-terms. In comparison to
\mathbf{pSKI}
, we have dropped the non-affine combinator
S
and added the combinators
B
and
C
, which represent the affine
\lambda
-terms

B=\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,x\,(y\,z),\qquad C=\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,\lambda z.\,(x\,z)\,y.

The operational rules for
B
and
C
(and their respective primed versions) are given in
Figure

2
, and the rules for all remaining operations are the same as for
\mathbf{pSKI}
(
Figure

1
). As before, the operational model of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
is the least higher-order coalgebra

(3.6)

\gamma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\colon\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\to B(\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}},\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}})

satisfying all operational rules of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
. This time, rather than contextual equivalence, we consider the
contextual pseudometric

(
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
)
for
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, which is the pseudometric on
\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
defined by

d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t,s)=\sup_{C[\cdot]\text{ context}}|\gamma(C[t])(\bot)-\gamma(C[s])(\bot)|.

Thus
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t,s)
measures to what extent contexts are able to distinguish
t
from
s
by observing termination probabilities. At this stage, the contextual pseudometric is easily defined but hard to use (the reader may try to prove the ‘obvious’ statement
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(I,I\oplus\Omega)=\frac{1}{2}
). Again, our categorical congruence results will lead to efficient coinductive proof methods that simplify the reasoning.

\displaystyle~B\Downarrow 1\cdot(t\mapsto B^{\prime}(t))~~~\qquad~B^{\prime}(t)\Downarrow 1\cdot(s\mapsto B^{\prime\prime}(t,s))~~~\qquad~B^{\prime\prime}(t,s)\Downarrow 1\cdot(u\mapsto t\,(s\,u))~~~

\displaystyle~C\Downarrow 1\cdot(t\mapsto C^{\prime}(t))~~~\qquad~C^{\prime}(t)\Downarrow 1\cdot(s\mapsto C^{\prime\prime}(t,s))~~~\qquad~C^{\prime\prime}(t,s)\Downarrow 1\cdot(u\mapsto(t\,u)\,s)~~~

Figure 2
.
Operational semantics of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
.

4.
Abstract Higher-Order Specifications

The languages
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
exemplify the way in which higher-order languages are typically specified: their syntax is given by a signature of operation symbols, and their operational semantics determines some form of transition system (higher-order coalgebra) on the set of program terms whose transitions are specified by a set of operational rules. This process is captured in categorical terms via the notion of
abstract higher-order specification
(
AHOS
), introduced next. AHOS are related to both
biGSOS

(
Rot 2019
)
and
higher-order abstract GSOS

(
Goncharov et al. 2023
)
specifications; see
4.14
.

Definition 4.1 (AHOS).

An
abstract higher-order specification
(
AHOS
)
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho)
is given by:

(1)

a category
\mathbb{B}
;

(2)

a functor
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
with an initial algebra
(\Lambda,\iota)
and a free algebra
\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda
generated by
\Lambda
.
2
2

2

The elements of
\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda
are thought of as terms whose variables are terms themselves.

(3)

a bifunctor
B\colon\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
with a cofree
B(\Lambda,-)
-coalgebra generated by
\Lambda
, denoted
B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
.

(4)

a morphism
\varrho\colon\Sigma B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\to B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)
, the
rule morphism
of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

A
model
of
{\mathcal{S}}
is a higher-order coalgebra
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
such that the following diagram commutes:

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B({\mathsf{id}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}})}

Here

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}

and

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}

are the cofree extension and free extension of
{\mathsf{id}}\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda
, respectively.

Informally,
\Sigma
and
B
specify the syntax and the behaviour type of a higher-order language. The initial
\Sigma
-algebra
\Lambda
is the object of program terms. The rule morphism
\varrho
describes the set of operational rules of the language and encodes them into a function. For each operation symbol
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
in the signature of the language,
\varrho
specifies the behaviour of a program
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
in terms of the unravelled (infinite-depth) behaviours of its subprograms
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, which are elements of the cofree coalgebra
B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
. A model of an AHOS is a transition system on the object
\Lambda
of program terms that ‘runs’ programs according to the operational rules specified by the map
\varrho
.

Remark 4.2.

The rule morphism
\varrho
of an AHOS typically extends to a dinatural transformation

\varrho_{X}\colon\Sigma B^{\infty}(X,X)\to B(X,\Sigma^{\star}X)\qquad(X\in\mathbb{B}).

Dinaturality amounts to the requirement that the operational rules encoded by
\varrho
are parametrically polymorphic, i.e. do not inspect the structure of their arguments. For instance, this is the case for the rules of
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
. In our congruence results for behavioural conformances (
Section

6
), parametric polymorphism is not expressed via dinaturality, but in terms of a lifting property of
\varrho
.

AHOS generally allow to specify the behaviour of a program
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
in terms of the infinite-depth behaviours of its subprograms
t_{i}
. In applications, it is typically sufficient to restrict to behaviours of some finite depth. For instance, in
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, the operational rule for an application
t\,s
(
Figure

1
) requires inspecting the depth-
2
behaviour of
t
, and the rules for all remaining operations inspect at most the depth-
1
behaviour of the operands. This is captured by the notion of
depth-
n
AHOS
defined below. We first introduce some required notation.

Notation 4.3.

Given an AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho)
we denote the counit and the
B(\Lambda,-)
-coalgebra structure of the cofree coalgebra
B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
by

\varepsilon\colon B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\to\Lambda\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau\colon B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\to B(\Lambda,B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)).

For each
n\geq 0
we write
B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
for the
n
-fold application of
B(\Lambda,-)
to
\Lambda
, that is,

B^{(0)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)=\Lambda\qquad\text{and}\qquad B^{(n+1)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)=B(\Lambda,B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)).

We define the projections

c^{(n)}\colon B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\to B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)

inductively by

c^{(0)}=\varepsilon\;\,\text{and}\;\,c^{(n+1)}=(\,B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\tau$}}\;}}$}}B(\Lambda,B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda))\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$B(\Lambda,c^{(n)})$}}\;}}$}}B(\Lambda,B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda))=B^{(n+1)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\,).

Finally, we define the
n
-fold iteration
\gamma^{(n)}\colon\Lambda\to B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
of
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
inductively by

\displaystyle\gamma^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(\,\Lambda\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize${\mathsf{id}}$}}\;}}$}}\Lambda=B^{(0)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\,)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=(\,\Lambda\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\gamma$}}\;}}$}}B(\Lambda,\Lambda)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$B(\Lambda,\gamma^{(n)})$}}\;}}$}}B(\Lambda,B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda))=B^{(n+1)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\,).

An easy induction on
n
shows that:

Lemma 4.4.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, one has
\gamma^{(n)}=(\,\Lambda\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}$}}\;}}$}}B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$c^{(n)}$}}\;}}$}}B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\,)
.

Definition 4.5 (Depth-
n
AHOS).

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A
depth-
n
AHOS
is a quadruple
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho)
where
\mathbb{B}
,
\Sigma
,
B
are given as for AHOS, the category
\mathbb{B}
has finite products, and
\varrho
is a morphism

(4.2)

\varrho\colon\Sigma\textstyle\prod_{k=0}^{n}B^{(n)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\to B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda).

A
model
of
{\mathcal{S}}
is a higher-order bialgebra
(\Lambda,\gamma)
such that the following diagram commutes:

(4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\prod_{k=0}^{n}B^{(k)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\langle\gamma^{(k)}\rangle_{k=0}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B({\mathsf{id}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}})}

Remark 4.6.

Every depth-
n
AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho)
induces the AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho^{\prime})
given by

\varrho^{\prime}=(\,\hbox to331.96pt{\vbox to30.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 165.97803pt\lower-13.09924pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

By
Lemma

4.4
, every model of
{\mathcal{S}}
is a model of
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, and vice versa. Therefore, depth-
n
AHOS can be regarded as a special case of AHOS. Depth-
n
AHOS are easier to work with as they do not involve the cofree coalgebra
B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
, which may be hard to compute or even fail to exist.

Example 4.7 (AHOS for
\mathbf{pSKI}
).

The language
\mathbf{pSKI}
is modelled by the depth-
2
AHOS

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pSKI}}=(\mathbf{Set},\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}},B,\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}})

where
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
is the polynomial functor corresponding to the signature (
3.1
) of
\mathbf{pSKI}
, and
B
is the behaviour bifunctor (
3.2
). To define the rule map
\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
, we write
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
for the set of closed
\mathbf{pSKI}
-terms, and note that

B^{(0)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)=\Lambda,\qquad B^{(1)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)={\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}),\qquad B^{(2)}(\Lambda,\Lambda)={\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda}).

Thus
\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
is a map of type

\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}\colon\Sigma(\Lambda\times{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})\times{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda}))\to{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+(\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)^{\Lambda})

that simply presents the operational rules of
Figure

1
:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(t\mapsto S^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(t\mapsto K^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle S^{\prime}((t,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(s\mapsto S^{\prime\prime}(t,s))

\displaystyle K^{\prime}((t,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(s\mapsto t)

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}((t,-,-),(s,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(u\mapsto(t\,u)\,(s\,u))

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(t\mapsto t)

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot\bot

\displaystyle(-,\varphi,-)\oplus(-,\psi,-)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\psi

\displaystyle\mathsf{app}((-,-,\Phi),(s,-,-))\;\;

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i}\cdot p_{i,j}\cdot b_{i,j},

where
t,s\in\Lambda
and
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})
, and in the last clause,

\Phi=p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot F_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda})\quad\text{and}\quad F_{i}(s)=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}p_{i,j}\cdot b_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}).

Remark 4.8.

In the clause for
S
, the expression
S^{\prime}(t)
appearing in the map
(t\mapsto S^{\prime}(t))\in(\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)^{\Lambda}
is to be read as a
\Sigma
-term in the variable
t\in\Lambda
,
not
as its interpretation in the initial
\Sigma
-algebra
\Lambda
, i.e. an element of
\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda
. Analogously for the terms
S^{\prime\prime}(t,s)
,
(t\,u)\,(s\,u)
,
K^{\prime}(t)
in the other clauses.

Remark 4.9.

The action of
\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
on
\mathsf{app}
-terms can be expressed in terms of the strength
\mathsf{st}
, the unit
\eta
and the multiplication
\mu
of the distribution monad
{\mathcal{D}}
(
Section

2
). Indeed, it is given by

\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}(\mathsf{app}((-,-,\Phi),(s,-,-))=j(\Phi,s)

where
j\colon B(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))\times\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
is the composite shown below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda})\times\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}((\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda})\times\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}\times\Lambda+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}\times\Lambda+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda}\times\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})+{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mu$}}\;}}$}}{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{st}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathsf{outl}}+{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}(\eta\circ{\mathsf{inl}}+{\mathsf{id}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}\nabla}

Example 4.10 (AHOS for
\mathbf{pBCKI}
).

The language
\mathbf{pBCKI}
is modelled by the depth-
2
AHOS

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}=(\mathbf{Set},\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}},B,\varrho_{\mathbf{pBCKI}})

where
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is the polynomial functor corresponding to the signature (
3.5
) of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, and
B
is again the behaviour bifunctor (
3.2
).
Putting
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
, the rule map

\varrho_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\colon\Sigma(\Lambda\times{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})\times{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda}))\to{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+(\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)^{\Lambda})

is given like in
Example

4.7
for
\Omega
,
K
,
K^{\prime}
,
I
,
\oplus
,
\mathsf{app}
, and for
B
,
B^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime\prime}
,
C
,
C^{\prime}
,
C^{\prime\prime}
by

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(t\mapsto B^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(t\mapsto C^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle B^{\prime}((t,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(s\mapsto B^{\prime\prime}(t,s))\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle C^{\prime}((t,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(s\mapsto C^{\prime\prime}(t,s))

\displaystyle B^{\prime\prime}((t,-,-),(s,-,-))\;\;

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(u\mapsto t\,(s\,u))

\displaystyle C^{\prime\prime}((t,-,-),(s,-,-))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\;\;1\cdot(u\mapsto(t\,u)\,s)

It remains to explain how to construct a model
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
of an AHOS. Similar to the construction of the operational models of the languages
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
in
Section

3
, the idea is that the
least
model is the one to be aimed for, since it carries precisely the information given by the operational rules of the AHOS, and nothing more.
This idea can be formalized by imposing a suitable order structure on the set
\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))
of higher-order coalgebras on program terms.

Definition 4.11 (Complete AHOS, Canonical Model).

An
ordered AHOS

{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
is an AHOS
(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\varrho)
together with a partial order
\leq
on the set
\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))
. The AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}
is
complete
if the order
\leq
is a DCPO with a least element
\bot
, and moreover the map

\Gamma\colon\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))\to\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda)),\qquad\gamma\mapsto B({\mathsf{id}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}})\circ\varrho\circ\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}\circ\iota^{-1},

is monotone. The
canonical model
of a complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}
is the
\leq
-least model of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Remark 4.12.

(1)

Since models of
{\mathcal{S}}
are precisely fixed points of the map
\Gamma
, the canonical model exists by the Knaster-Tarski fixed point theorem.

(2)

The canonical model can be constructed iteratively (which corresponds to the iterative proof of the Knaster-Tarski theorem). Consider the ordinal-indexed family of higher-order coalgebras
(\gamma_{\alpha}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda))_{\alpha}
defined via transfinite recursion as follows:

\gamma_{0}=\bot,\qquad\gamma_{\alpha+1}=B({\mathsf{id}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}})\circ\varrho\circ\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}_{\alpha}\circ\iota^{-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma_{\alpha}=\bigvee_{\beta<\alpha}\gamma_{\beta}\;\;\;(\text{$\alpha$ limit ordinal}).

These coalgebras form an ascending chain (
\alpha\leq\beta
implies
\gamma_{\alpha}\leq\gamma_{\beta}
). Since the hom-set
\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))
is small, the chain eventually stabilizes, that is, there exists an ordinal
\alpha_{0}
such that
\gamma_{\alpha}=\gamma_{\alpha_{0}}
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
. Then
\gamma=\gamma_{\alpha_{0}}
is the least fixed point of
\Gamma
, i.e. the canonical model of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Intuitively, this iteration computes the canonical model
\gamma
step by step from below: One starts with a trivial coalgebra
\gamma_{0}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
that carries no information, and then constructs a sequence
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3},\cdots
of increasingly more detailed approximations of
\gamma
, until a fixed point is reached.

Example 4.13 (
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
).

The AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
modelling the language
\mathbf{pSKI}
(
Example

4.7
) is complete w.r.t. the pointwise DCPO structure on
\mathbf{Set}(\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}},{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}^{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}}))
. The map
\Gamma
of
Definition

4.11
is precisely the map (
3.4
); we have already observed in
Section

3
that this map is monotone since the operational rules of
\mathbf{pSKI}
are. Thus, the canonical model of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
is the higher-order coalgebra
\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
defined in (
3.3
). Similarly, the canonical model of the complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{BCKI}}
(
Example

4.10
) is the higher-order coalgebra
\gamma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
given by (
3.6
).

We conclude this section with a discussion of existing categorical frameworks related to AHOS.

Remark 4.14.

AHOS form a common generalization of two operational specification frameworks known in the literature, namely
biGSOS

(
Rot 2019
)
and
higher-order abstract GSOS

(
Goncharov et al. 2023
)
. In more detail:

(1)

AHOS are a higher-order version of
monotone biGSOS specifications

(
Rot 2019
)
. The latter are natural transformations of type
\Sigma B^{\infty}\to B\Sigma^{\star}
for an endofunctor (rather than mixed variance bifunctor)
B
. Our construction of the operational model of a complete AHOS as a least fixed point extends a corresponding construction from
op. cit.
to the higher-order setting.

(2)

AHOS also relate to the recently introduced
higher-order abstract GSOS
framework
(
Goncharov et al. 2023
)
. The core ingredient of the latter is the notion of
higher-order GSOS law
, which is essentially the same as a depth-
1
AHOS (subject to additional dinaturality conditions). To construct the operational model of a higher-order GSOS law, no order on
B
is necessary: there is a
unique
higher-order coalgebra
\gamma
making (
4.3
) with
n=1
commute, and it can be defined via structural induction (using initiality of the algebra
\Lambda=\mu\Sigma
) rather than as an order-theoretic least fixed point. This is no longer true for depth-
n
AHOS where
n\geq 2
. The present notion of AHOS can thus be regarded as a substantially more general and flexible version of higher-order abstract GSOS. In particular, due to restriction to operational models definable by structural induction, the latter framework is inherently tailored to small-step operational semantics. Operational rules corresponding to a big-step semantics can only be captured indirectly in higher-order abstract GSOS, namely by generating them from a small-step specification. This requires syntactic restrictions on the format, such as an explicit syntactic distinction between values and computations, and a complex technical condition on higher-order GSOS laws called
strong separatedness

(
Goncharov et al. 2025b
)
. In contrast, our present AHOS framework captures big-step operational semantics directly, and with a remarkably simple categorical setup. The operational model of an AHOS is, in contrast to the approach of higher-order abstract GSOS, constructed via a form of rule induction (corresponding to the order-theoretic construction of a least fixed point), not via the more restrictive structural induction. It is the use of rule induction that allows modelling rules that require unravelling more than one layer of behaviours (like the rule for application in
Figure

1
), which is typical for big-step specifications.

With the abstract categorical account of higher-order languages and their operational semantics provided by AHOS at hand, our primary goal is to reason about notions of
behavioural conformance
(e.g. behavioural equivalence or behavioural distance) on canonical models of AHOS. This is achieved by putting suitable fibrations over the base category of an AHOS.

5.
Fibrations and Behavioural Conformances

Behavioural conformances for transition systems, and coalgebras in general, can be conveniently modelled by considering liftings of behaviour functors along fibrations
(
Hermida and Jacobs 1998
;
Baldan et al. 2018
)
. The level of abstraction achieved in this way gives rise to a uniform framework for reasoning about similarity of programs, bisimilarity of programs, distances of programs, and related notions. In this section, we set up the fibrational foundations for our theory of behavioural conformances on canonical models of AHOS. In particular, we develop a fibrational version of
Howe’s closure
, the key construction for proving abstract congruence results for higher-order languages.

5.1.
Complete Lattice Fibrations

We first recall some terminology and basic results from the theory of fibrations; see
Jacobs 1999
for a comprehensive introduction to the subject. In the context of the coalgebraic theory of behavioural conformances, a fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is thought of as the forgetful functor of a category
{\mathbb{E}}
of
\mathbb{B}
-objects carrying additional (typically relational) structure, together with a notion of pulling back that structure along maps in the base category
\mathbb{B}
.

Definition 5.1 (Fibration).

Let
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
be a functor.

(1)

An object
P\in{\mathbb{E}}
is
above
the object
X\in\mathbb{B}
if
pP=X
; similarly, a morphism
u\in{\mathbb{E}}
is
above
the morphism
f\in\mathbb{B}
if
pu=f
. The
fiber
at
X\in\mathbb{B}
is the non-full subcategory
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{E}}
given by all objects above
X
and all morphisms above
{\mathsf{id}}_{X}
.

(2)

Given
X\in\mathbb{B}
and
P\in{\mathbb{E}}
, a
cartesian lift
of a morphism
f\colon X\to pP
is a morphism
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f\colon f^{\star}P\to P
above
f
such that for every morphism
g\colon Q\to P
in
{\mathbb{E}}
and
h\colon pQ\to X
such that
pg=f\circ h
, there exists a unique
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$h$}}}}h\colon Q\to f^{\star}P
above
h
such that
g=\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f\circ\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$h$}}}}h
. The functor
p
is a
fibration
if every
f\colon X\to pP
has a cartesian lift. Every
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
induces the
reindexing
functor

f^{\star}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{X},\qquad P\mapsto f^{\star}P.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pQ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pP}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle h$}}}}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle f$}}}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(3)

Dually, an
opcartesian lift
of
f\colon pP\to Y
is a morphism
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f\colon P\to f_{\star}P
above
f
such that for every morphism
g\colon P\to Q
in
{\mathbb{E}}
and
h\colon Y\to pQ
such that
pg=h\circ f
, there exists a unique
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$h$}}}}h\colon f_{\star}P\to Q
above
h
such that
g=\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$h$}}}}h\circ\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f
. The functor
p
is an
opfibration
if every
f\colon pP\to Y
has an opcartesian lift. Every
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
induces the
opreindexing
functor

f_{\star}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{Y},\qquad P\mapsto f_{\star}P.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pP}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pQ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle f$}}}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.5pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle h$}}}}h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

(4)

The functor
p
is a
bifibration
if it is both a fibration and an opfibration.

(5)

A fibration
p
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
if each fiber is a complete lattice, i.e. a small poset (viewed as a thin category) where each subset has a join (= least upper bound) and a meet (= greatest lower bound), and moreover the reindexing map
f^{\star}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
is meet-preserving for every
f\colon X\to Y
. We denote the partial order on
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
by
\sqsubseteq_{X}
, or simply
\sqsubseteq
, and joins and meets in
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
by
\bigsqcup
and
\bigsqcap
.

The next lemma collects some standard facts about
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibrations:

Lemma 5.2.

For every
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, the following statements hold:

(1)

p
is faithful, that is, for all
u,v\colon P\to Q
in
{\mathbb{E}}
, if
pu=pv
then
u=v
.

(2)

p
is a bifibration.

(3)

For each
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
there is a Galois connection
f_{\star}\dashv f^{\star}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
, that is,

f_{\star}P\sqsubseteq Q\iff P\sqsubseteq f^{\star}Q\qquad\text{for all $P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{X}$ and $Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}$}.

In particular, the map
f_{\star}
preserves all joins.

(4)

One has
(g\circ f)_{\star}=g_{\star}\circ f_{\star}
and
(g\circ f)^{\star}=f^{\star}\circ g^{\star}
for all
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
in
\mathbb{B}
.

Remark 5.3.

\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibrations are essentially equivalent to another notion from category theory, namely
topological functors

(
Adámek et al. 1990
)
; in fact, a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration is precisely a topological functor with small fibers. This observation is folklore and has been noted by several authors without a proof
(
Komorida et al. 2019
;
Forster et al. 2024
)
. We give a proof in the appendix (
Appendix

A
) for the convenience of the reader.

Example 5.4 (
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-Fibrations).

(1)

To capture (bi)simulations in the fibrational setting, we consider a fibration of relations. A
relation
is a pair
(X,R)
of a set
X
and a subset
R\subseteq X\times X
. Given two relations
(X,R)
and
(Y,S)
, a pair
(f,g)
of maps
f,g\colon X\to Y
is
jointly relation-preserving
if
R(x,x^{\prime})
implies
S(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
. We let
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
denote the category of relations and jointly relation-preserving pairs of maps. The forgetful functor
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
(cf.
Section

2
,
Notation
) given by
(X,d)\mapsto X
and
(f,g)\mapsto(f,g)
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration.
We denote the fiber at
X\in\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
by
\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X}
. The (op)reindexing maps for
(f,g)\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
are given by

\displaystyle(f,g)^{\star}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},Y}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X},

\displaystyle\quad(Y,S)\mapsto(X,(f,g)^{\star}S),

\displaystyle(f,g)_{\star}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},Y},

\displaystyle\quad(X,R)\mapsto(Y,(f,g)_{\star}R),

where
(f,g)^{\star}S
and
(f,g)_{\star}R
are formed by taking the (pre)image under the map
f\times g\colon X\times X\to Y\times Y
:

(f,g)^{\star}S=(f\times g)^{-1}[S]\qquad\text{and}\qquad(f,g)_{\star}R=(f\times g)[R].

The order
\sqsubseteq
on a fiber
\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X}
is given by inclusion of relations:
(X,R)\sqsubseteq(X,S)
iff
R\subseteq S
.

(2)

Given two relations
(X,R)
and
(Y,S)
, a map
f\colon X\to Y
is
relation-preserving
if
R(x,x^{\prime})
implies
S(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, that is, if
(f,f)
is jointly relation-preserving. We let
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}
denote the category of relations and relation-preserving maps. The forgetful functor
p_{\mathbf{rp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}\to\mathbf{Set}
given by
(X,R)\mapsto X
and
f\mapsto f
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration. The (op)reindexing maps for
f\colon X\to Y
are given in terms of those for
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}
by
f^{\star}=(f,f)^{\star}
and
f_{\star}=(f,f)_{\star}
.

(3)

Quantitative notions of behavioural distance are captured by moving from relations to fuzzy relations. A
fuzzy relation
is a pair
(X,d)
of a set
X
and a map
d\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
. Given two fuzzy relations
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
, a pair
(f,g)
of maps
f,g\colon X\to Y
is
jointly non-expansive
if
d_{Y}(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
. We let
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
denote the category of fuzzy relations and jointly non-expansive pairs of maps. The forgetful functor
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
given by
(X,d)\mapsto X
and
(f,g)\mapsto(f,g)
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration. The (op)reindexing maps for
(f,g)\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
are given by

\displaystyle(f,g)^{\star}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},Y}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X},

\displaystyle\quad(Y,d)\mapsto(X,(f,g)^{\star}d),

\displaystyle(f,g)_{\star}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},Y},

\displaystyle\quad(X,d)\mapsto(Y,(f,g)_{\star}d),

with the fuzzy relations
(f,g)^{\star}d
and
(f,g)_{\star}d
defined as follows:

(f,g)^{\star}d(x,x^{\prime})=d(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))\quad\text{and}\quad(f,g)_{\star}d(y,y^{\prime})=\inf\{\,d(x,x^{\prime})\mid f(x)=y,\,g(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}\,\}.

The order
\sqsubseteq
on a fiber
\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X}
is given by the
reversed
pointwise order of fuzzy relations, that is,
(X,d)\sqsubseteq(X,d^{\prime})
iff
d(x,x)\geq d^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
.

(4)

Given fuzzy relations
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
, a map
f\colon X\to Y
is
non-expansive
if
d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, that is, if the pair
(f,f)
is jointly non-expansive. The forgetful functor
p_{\mathbf{ne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}\to\mathbf{Set}
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration. The (op)reindexing maps for
f\colon X\to Y
are given in terms of those for
p_{\mathbf{jne}}
by
f^{\star}=(f,f)^{\star}
and
f_{\star}=(f,f)_{\star}
.

Remark 5.5.

(1)

A relation
R\subseteq X\times X
is identified with the fuzzy relation
d\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
given by
d(x,x^{\prime})=0
if
R(x,x^{\prime})
and
d(x,x^{\prime})=1
otherwise. Under this identification, jointly relation-preserving pairs of maps correspond precisely to jointly non-expansive pairs of maps, so
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
forms a full subcategory of
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}
forms a full subcategory of
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}
.

(2)

In fibrational terminology,
p_{\mathbf{rp}}
and
p_{\mathbf{ne}}
arise from
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}
and
p_{\mathbf{jne}}
via
change of base

(
Jacobs 1999
, Lem. 1.5.1)
, that is, the diagrams below are pullbacks in the (superlarge) category of categories and functors. Here
I
is the inclusion functor (
Section

2
,
Notation
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbf{rp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbf{jrp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbf{ne}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\mathbf{jne}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

(3)

In most accounts of behavioural conformances for coalgebras, the authors work with fibrations like
p_{\mathbf{rp}}
and
p_{\mathbf{ne}}
. The slight extension to
jointly
relation-preserving or non-expansive pairs of maps is required for our fibrational development of Howe’s method.

5.2.
Idempotent Quantale Fibrations

Apart from being a complete lattice, the fiber
\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X}
(
=\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{rp}},X}
) naturally carries an algebraic structure whose operations are given by composition and reversal of relations:

R\cdot S=\{\,(x,y)\mid\exists z\in Z.\,R(x,z)\wedge S(z,y)\,\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R^{\circ}=\{\,(y,x)\mid R(x,y)\,\}.

Similarly for the fiber
\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X}
(
=\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{ne}},X}
), where the fuzzy generalizations of composition and reversal are given as follows (we write
a\boxplus b=\min\{a+b,1\}
for truncated addition in
[0,1]
):

\displaystyle d\cdot e\colon X\times X\to[0,1],

\displaystyle(d\cdot e)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{z\in Z}\{\,d(x,z)\boxplus e(z,y)\,\},

\displaystyle d^{\circ}\colon X\times X\to[0,1],

\displaystyle d^{\circ}(x,y)

\displaystyle=d(y,x).

In both cases, composition and reversal are compatible with the complete lattice structure of the fibers and with reindexing. This is captured abstractly by the following definitions:

Definition 5.6.

A
monoid

(M,\cdot,1)
is a set
M
equipped with a

binary operation
\cdot\colon M\times M\to M
and a constant
1\in M
satisfying (
5.1
) and (
5.2
). A
involutive monoid
(or
\circ
-monoid
) is a monoid
M
equipped with an additional unary operation
(-)^{\circ}\colon M\to M
satisfying (
5.3
)–(
5.5
). A
quantale
is a monoid
M
equipped with complete lattice structure satisfying (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) for all
x\in M
and
S\subseteq M
. An
involutive quantale
is a monoid that is both involutive and a quantale, and additionally satisfies (
5.8
). A
morphism

h\colon M\to N
of (
\circ
-)monoids is a map satisfying, for all
x,y\in M
,

1=h(1)\quad\text{and}\quad h(x)\cdot h(y)=h(x\cdot y)\quad(\text{and}\quad(h(x))^{\circ}=h(x^{\circ})).

If
N
is equipped with a partial order
\sqsubseteq
(e.g. if
N
is a quantale), a
lax (
\circ
-)morphism
is a map
h\colon M\to N
satisfying

1\sqsubseteq h(1)\quad\text{and}\quad h(x)\cdot h(y)\sqsubseteq h(x\cdot y)\quad(\text{and}\quad(h(x))^{\circ}\sqsubseteq h(x^{\circ})).

(5.1)

\displaystyle(x\cdot y)\cdot z

\displaystyle=x\cdot(y\cdot z)

(5.2)

\displaystyle 1\cdot x=x

\displaystyle=x\cdot 1

(5.3)

\displaystyle(x^{\circ})^{\circ}

\displaystyle=x

(5.4)

\displaystyle 1^{\circ}

\displaystyle=1,

(5.5)

\displaystyle(x\cdot y)^{\circ}

\displaystyle=y^{\circ}\cdot x^{\circ}

(5.6)

\displaystyle x\cdot\bigsqcup S

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}x\cdot s

(5.7)

\displaystyle(\bigsqcup S)\cdot x

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}s\cdot x

(5.8)

\displaystyle(\bigsqcup S)^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}s^{\circ}

Definition 5.7.

A
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is a
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
if

each fiber
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
(
X\in\mathbb{B}
) carries a quantale structure
({\mathbb{E}}_{X},\cdot,1_{X})
satisfying (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) for each
f\colon X\to Y
and
P,Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}
. It is an
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration if each fiber carries an involutive quantale structure satisfying (
5.9
)–(
5.11
).

(5.9)

\displaystyle 1_{X}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq f^{\star}1_{Y}

(5.10)

\displaystyle f^{\star}P\cdot f^{\star}Q

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq f^{\star}(P\cdot Q)

(5.11)

\displaystyle(f^{\star}P)^{\circ}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq f^{\star}(P^{\circ})

Definition 5.8.

A
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}^{(2)}
is a
heterogeneous

(\mathbf{In})\mathbf{Qtl}
