-fibration if each fiber carries an (involutive) quantale structure
({\mathbb{E}}_{X},\cdot,1_{X})
such that, for
f,g,h\colon X\to Y
and
P,Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}
,

(5.12)

1_{X}\sqsubseteq(f,f)^{\star}1_{Y}\quad\text{and}\quad(f,g)^{\star}P\cdot(g,h)^{\star}Q\sqsubseteq(f,h)^{\star}(P\cdot Q)\quad(\text{and}\quad((f,f)^{\star}P)^{\circ}=(f,f)^{\star}(P^{\circ})).

Heterogeneous
(\mathbf{In})\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibrations provide exactly enough structure to make Howe’s method work. Hence, this is the level of generality at which our congruence results for AHOS will emerge.

Example 5.9.

(1)

The fibration
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
of
Example

5.4
(1)
is a heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration. For each set
X
the involutive quantale structure
(\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X},\cdot,1_{X},(-)^{\circ})
is given by composition of relations, the identity relation
1_{X}\subseteq X\times X
, and reversal of relations.

(2)

The fibration
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
of
Example

5.4
(3)
is a heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration. For each set
X
the involutive quantale structure
(\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X},\cdot,1_{X},(-)^{\circ})
is given by composition of fuzzy relations, the identity relation
1_{X}\subseteq X\times X
viewed as a fuzzy relation, and the reversal operator.

Definition 5.10.

An element
x
of a quantale is
reflexive
if
1\sqsubseteq x
, and
transitive
if
x\cdot x\sqsubseteq x
. If the quantale is involutive,
x
is
symmetric
if
x^{\circ}=x
(equivalently
x^{\circ}\sqsubseteq x
). The
transitive closure
of
x
is given by
x^{+}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}x^{n}
, where
x^{n}=x\cdot\cdots\cdot x
denotes the
n
-fold product.

Note that a reflexive, symmetric, and transitive element of
\mathbf{Rel}_{{\mathbf{jrp}},X}
and
\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X}
is, respectively, an equivalence relation and a pseudometric on
X
.

5.3.
Lifting Functors Along Fibrations

The key parameter to the fibrational notion of behavioural conformance for coalgebras is the choice of a lifting of the behaviour functor along a fibration. Dually, different notions of
congruence
for algebras are modelled in terms of liftings of the syntax functor.

Definition 5.11 (Lifting).

A
lifting
of an endofunctor
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
(a bifunctor
F\colon\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
, a mixed variance bifunctor
F\colon\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
) along the fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is functor
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
on
{\mathbb{E}}
making the respective diagram below commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}\times{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\times p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\mathsf{op}}\times p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

If each fiber is a quantale, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
is
(laxly) monoidal
if for each
X,Y\in\mathbb{B}
the induced map

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{FX}\qquad/\qquad\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\times{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{F(X,Y)}\qquad/\qquad\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{F(X,Y)}

between fibers is a (lax) monoid morphism. Similarly, if each fiber is an an involutive quantale, a lifting is
(laxly)
\circ
-monoidal
if the above induced maps between fibers are (lax)
\circ
-morphisms.

Remark 5.12.

A lifting of a functor along a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration is uniquely determined by its action on objects since the fibration is faithful (
Lemma

5.2
(1)
).

Example 5.13 (Fuzzy Relation Liftings).

We present a number of liftings of functors on
\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
along the fibration
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
(
Example

5.4
(3)
). These liftings will feature in the categorical modelling of behavioural conformances for our example languages
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
.

(1)

The endofunctor
\{\bot\}+(-)\colon\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}

lifts to the endofunctor

(-)_{\bot}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}},\qquad(X,d)_{\bot}=(\{\bot\}+X,d_{\bot}),

where
d_{\bot}(x,x^{\prime})=d(x,x^{\prime})
for
x,x^{\prime}\in X
,
d_{\bot}(\bot,\overline{x})=0
for
\overline{x}\in\{\bot\}+X
, and
d_{\bot}(x,\bot)=1
for
x\in X
.

(2)

The product and coproduct functors
\times,+\colon\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\times\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
(which are formed like in
\mathbf{Set}
) lift to the product and coproduct on
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
. Indeed, the product of fuzzy relations is given by

(X,d_{X})\times(Y,d_{Y})=(X\times Y,d_{X\times Y})\quad\text{where}\quad d_{X\times Y}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\max\{\,d_{X}(x,x^{\prime}),d_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})\,\},

and the coproduct by

(X,d_{X})+(Y,d_{Y})=(X+Y,d_{X+Y})

where
d_{X+Y}(z,z^{\prime})
is
d_{X}(z,z^{\prime})
or
d_{Y}(z,z^{\prime})
if
z,z^{\prime}
both lie in
X
or
Y
, respectively, and
1
otherwise.

(3)

An alternative lifting of the product functor
\times
from
\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
to
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
is given by

(X,d_{X})\,\overline{\times}\,(Y,d_{Y})=(X\times Y,d_{+})\quad\text{where}\quad d_{+}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\boxplus d_{Y}(y,y^{\prime}),

Recall that
\boxplus
denotes truncated addition.

(4)

The hom bifunctor

H\colon(\mathbf{Set}^{(2)})^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)},\qquad H(X,Y)=Y^{X},\qquad H((f,g),(h,k))=(h\circ-\circ f,k\circ-\circ g),

has a lifting given by

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}},\qquad\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H((X,d_{X}),(Y,d_{Y}))=(Y,d_{Y})^{(X,d_{X})}=(Y^{X},d_{Y^{X}}),

where the fuzzy relation
d_{Y^{X}}
on the function space
Y^{X}
is given by

d_{Y^{X}}(f,g)=\inf\{\,\varepsilon\in[0,1]\mid\forall x,x^{\prime}\in X.\,d_{Y}(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon\,\}.

Thus
d_{Y^{X}}(f,g)
measures the ‘defect’ of joint non-expansivity of the pair
(f,g)
. Note that the infimum is attained, i.e. one has

d_{Y}(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))\leq d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})+d_{Y^{X}}(f,g)\quad\text{for all $x,x^{\prime}\in X$},

which means precisely that the evaluation map

{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}}\colon(Y^{X},d_{Y^{X}})\,\overline{\times}\,(X,d_{X})\to(Y,d_{Y}),\quad(f,x)\mapsto f(x),

is non-expansive. Indeed,
\overline{\times}
is a closed monoidal structure with internal homs given by
(Y,d_{Y})^{(X,d_{X})}
.

(5)

Let
{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
be the countable distribution functor, and consider its corresponding functor
{\mathcal{D}}^{(2)}\colon\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
. The
Wasserstein lifting

(
Baldan et al. 2018
)

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{(2)}
is given by

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)=({\mathcal{D}}X,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d)

where
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d
is the
Wasserstein distance
on
{\mathcal{D}}X
: given
\varphi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and
\varphi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{D}}X
, the distance
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is the value of a minimum solution of the (countably infinite) linear program

shown on the right. Note that a minimum solution always exists
(
Kortanek and Yamasaki 1995
, Thm. 2.1)
. We refer to this linear program as the
transportation problem from
\varphi
to
\varphi^{\prime}
. One interprets
p_{i}
as the supply of some commodity available at location
x_{i}
, and
p_{j}^{\prime}
as the demand for that commodity at location
x_{j}^{\prime}
, and
d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})
as the cost of transporting one unit of the commodity from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}^{\prime}
. A solution
(t_{i,j})_{i\in I,j\in J}

\begin{array}[]{ll@{}ll}\text{minimize}&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}&\,d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot t_{i,j}&\\
\text{subject to}&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}&\,t_{i,j}=p_{i}&(i\in I)\\
&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}&\,t_{i,j}=p_{j}^{\prime}&(j\in J)\\
&&\,t_{i,j}\geq 0&(i\in I,\,j\in J)\end{array}

of the problem is a transportation plan that determines the amount
t_{i,j}
of the commodity to be transported from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}^{\prime}
for each
i,j
and requires that all supplies and demands are matched. The minimum value of the transportation problem is the cost of a cheapest transportation plan.

Let us mention a few useful properties of the Wasserstein distance:

Lemma 5.14.

Let
(X,d),(Y,d^{\prime})
be fuzzy relations.

(1)

For all
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}X
and
p\in[0,1]
we have

(5.13)

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d(p\cdot\varphi+(1-p)\cdot\psi,\,p\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot\psi^{\prime})\leq p\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})+(1-p)\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d(\psi,\psi^{\prime}).

(2)

The map
\mathsf{st}\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)\,\overline{\times}\,(Y,d^{\prime})\to\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((X,d)\,{\overline{\times}}\,(Y,d^{\prime}))
is non-expansive.

(3)

The map
\eta\colon(X,d)\to\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)
is non-expansive.

(4)

The map
\mu\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}\,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)\to\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)
is non-expansive.

Parts
(3)
and
(4)
imply that
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}
not only lifts
{\mathcal{D}}^{(2)}
as a functor, but as a
monad
.

Example 5.15 (Relation Liftings).

All liftings of
Example

5.13
restrict to liftings along the fibration
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
, where
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
is regarded as the full subcategory of
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
given by
\{0,1\}
-valued fuzzy relations. We denote the restricted liftings again by
(-)_{\bot}
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}
etc. More explicitly:

(1)

The relation lifting
(-)_{\bot}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of
\{\bot\}+(-)
maps the relation
(X,R)
to
(\{\bot\}+X,R_{\bot})
where
R_{\bot}
is given by
R_{\bot}(x,x^{\prime})
iff either
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
R(x,x^{\prime})
, or
x=\bot
.

(2)

The relation lifting
+\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\times\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of the coproduct maps the relations
(X,R)
and
(Y,S)
to
(X+Y,R+S)
. Similarly, the relation lifting
\times\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\times\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of the product maps
(X,R)
and
(Y,S)
to
(X\times Y,R\times S)
where
R\times S((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))
iff
R(x,x^{\prime})
and
S(y,y^{\prime})
.

(3)

The alternative lifting
\overline{\times}
of the product coincides with
\times
in the case of relations.

(4)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of the hom bifunctor
H
maps two relations
(X,R)
and
(Y,S)
to the relation
(Y^{X},S^{R})
where
S^{R}(f,g)
iff for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, if
R(x,x^{\prime})
then
S(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))
.

(5)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{(2)}
maps
(X,R)
to
({\mathcal{D}}X,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}R)
where
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}R(\sum_{i}p_{i}\cdot x_{i},\sum_{j}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime})
iff there exist non-negative reals
t_{i,j}\geq 0
(
i\in I
,
j\in J
) such that, for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
,

\sum_{j\in J:\,R(x_{i},x_{j})}t_{i,j}=p_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i\in I:\,R(x_{i},x_{j})}t_{i,j}=p_{j}^{\prime}.

This says that there exists a transportation plan sending goods only along connections given by
R
.

We conclude this subsection by noting an important property of
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibrations: free algebras and cofree coalgebras of lifted endofunctors can both be lifted from the base category. To the best of our knowledge, this result is new. In the case where
\mathbb{B}
has binary products and coproducts, it follows from a corresponding result for initial algebras and final coalgebras
(
Forster et al. 2024
, Lem. III.5)
.

Proposition 5.16 (Lifting of Free Algebras).

Let
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
be a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration, and suppose that
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
is an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along
p
. For every
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
, if
F
has a free algebra
F^{\star}X
generated by
X
, then
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
has a free algebra
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
generated by
P
. Moreover,

(1)

the structure and unit of
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
are above the structure and unit of
F^{\star}X
, respectively;

(2)

for every
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra
(A,a)
and
f\colon P\to A
in
{\mathbb{E}}
, the free extension
f^{\#}\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\to(A,a)
of
f
is above the free extension
(pf)^{\#}\colon F^{\star}X\to(pA,pa)
of
pf\colon X\to pA
.

A dual statement applies to cofree coalgebras
F^{\infty}X
.

5.4.
Behavioural Conformances and Biconformances

Liftings of behaviour functors along fibrations give rise to an abstract notion of (bi)simulation and its quantitative counterpart, behavioural distances
(
Hermida and Jacobs 1998
)
.

Notation 5.17.

Let
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
be a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration. We denote an object
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
by
(X,P)
, alluding to the intuition that
{\mathbb{E}}
-objects are
\mathbb{B}
-objects with extra structure, and call
P
a
conformance
over the object
X

(
Komorida et al. 2019
;
Beohar et al. 2024
)
. For
(X,P),(Y,Q)
in
{\mathbb{E}}
and
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
, we write
f\colon(X,P)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\boldsymbol{\cdot}$}}\;}}$}}(Y,Q)
if there exists a (necessarily unique) morphism
\dot{f}\colon(X,P)\to(Y,Q)
in
{\mathbb{E}}
above
f
. Note that

f\colon(X,P)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\boldsymbol{\cdot}$}}\;}}$}}(Y,Q)\;\;\iff\;\;P\sqsubseteq f^{\star}Q\;\;\iff\;\;f_{\star}P\sqsubseteq Q.

Definition 5.18 (Behavioural Conformance).

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
. An
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance
on an
F
-coalgebra
(C,c)
is a conformance
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
such that the following equivalent conditions hold:

c\colon(C,P)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\boldsymbol{\cdot}$}}\;}}$}}(FC,\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP)\;\;\iff\;\;P\sqsubseteq c^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP\;\;\iff\;\;c_{\star}P\sqsubseteq\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP.

If a
\sqsubseteq
-greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on
(C,c)
exists, we denote it by
P_{c}^{\to}
.

For
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibrations and under mild assumptions on the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
, the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance
P_{c}^{\to}
always exists:

Lemma 5.19.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a laxly monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and let
(C,c)
be an
F
-coalgebra.

(1)

The transitive closure of a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on
(C,c)
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance.

(2)

P_{c}^{\to}
is given by the join in
{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
of all
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances on
(C,c)
.

(3)

P_{c}^{\to}
is reflexive and transitive.

Example 5.20 (Simulations).

Consider the behaviour bifunctor
B(X,Y)={\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+Y^{X})
for
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, and the associated
endo
functor
F=B(L,-)\colon\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
that fixes the contravariant component of
B
to some set
L
of labels. Given a coalgebra
c\colon C\to FC
, we write
x\Downarrow\varphi
if
c(x)=\varphi
.

(1)

Let
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
be the following lifting of
F
along the fibration
p_{\mathbf{rp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}\to\mathbf{Set}
:

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F(X,R)=({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X^{L},\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((R^{L})_{\bot}))

where
(-)_{\bot}
is the lifting of
\{\bot\}+(-)
from
Example

5.15
and
R^{L}
is the
L
-fold product of the relation
(X,R)
. Then a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on a coalgebra
(C,c)
corresponds to a
probabilistic simulation
, i.e. a relation
R\subseteq C\times C
such that, for all
x,y\in C
,

R(x,y)\wedge x\Downarrow\varphi\wedge y\Downarrow\psi\implies\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((R^{L})_{\bot})(\varphi,\psi).

The conclusion
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((R^{L})_{\bot})(\varphi,\psi)
says that there exists a transportion plan from
\varphi
to
\psi
along
(R^{L})_{\bot}
.

(2)

We now extend the above above lifting of
F
to a lifting along the fibration
p_{\mathbf{ne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}\to\mathbf{Set}
:

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F(X,d)=({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X^{L},\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((d^{L})_{\bot}))

where
(-)_{\bot}
is the fuzzy relation lifting of
\{\bot\}+(-)
and
(X,d)^{L}=(X,d^{L})
is the
L
-fold product of
(X,d)
. Then a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on a coalgebra
(C,c)
is a
probabilistic fuzzy simulation
, i.e. a fuzzy relation
d\colon C\times C\to[0,1]
such that, for all
x,y\in C
,

(5.14)

x\Downarrow\varphi\wedge y\Downarrow\psi\implies\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((d^{L})_{\bot})(\varphi,\psi)\leq d(x,y).

The notions of probabilistic (fuzzy) simulation apply, in particular, to the operational models of
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, which are
F
-coalgebras
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
for
\Lambda\in\{\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}},\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\}
. Here
L=\Lambda
.

As indicated by the above examples, behavioural conformances are to be thought of as an abstract notion of simulation (or quantitative versions thereof). This is supported by the fact that the greatest behavioural conformance on a coalgebra is always a preorder (
Lemma

5.19
(3)
), but generally not symmetric. To achieve symmetry, we consider behavioural
bi
conformances, which are fibrational abstractions of
bi
simulations and naturally emerge in the setting of
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibrations:

Definition 5.21 (Behavioural Biconformance).

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
. An
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance
on an
F
-coalgebra
(C,c)
is a conformance
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
such that both
P
and
P^{\circ}
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances on
(C,c)
. If a
\sqsubseteq
-greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on
(C,c)
exists, we denote it by
P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
.

Much analogous to
Lemma

5.19
, we have:

Lemma 5.22.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a laxly monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and let
(C,c)
be an
F
-coalgebra.

(1)

P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
is given by the join in
{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
of all
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformances on
(C,c)
.

(2)

P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.

Example 5.23 (Bisimulations).

(1)

In the setting of
Example

5.20
(1)
, a

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on a coalgebra
(C,c)
corresponds to a
probabilistic bisimulation

R\subseteq C\times C
, where

R(x,y)\wedge x\Downarrow\varphi\wedge y\Downarrow\psi\implies\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((R^{L})_{\bot})(\varphi,\psi)\wedge\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((R^{\circ})^{L})_{\bot})(\psi,\varphi).

This notion essentially goes back to
Larsen and Skou 1991
, except that we do not insist on bisimulations to be equivalence relations. However, by
Lemma

5.22
,
probabilistic bisimilarity
(the greatest probabilistic bisimulation) is always an equivalence relation.

(2)

Similarly, in
Example

5.20
(2)
, a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on
(C,c)
is precisely a
probabilistic fuzzy bisimulation

d\colon C\times C\to[0,1]
, where in the conclusion of (
5.14
) we additionally require
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(((d^{\circ})^{L})_{\bot})(\psi,\varphi)\leq d(x,y)
. The greatest probabilistic fuzzy bisimulation on
(C,c)
is the
behavioural pseudometric

(
Baldan et al. 2018
)
on
(C,c)
; note that it is indeed a pseudometric by
Lemma

5.22
.

The notions of probabilistic bisimilarity and behavioural pseudometric apply to the canonical models of
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, and correspond to analogous notions for probabilistic
\lambda
-calculi
(
Lago et al. 2014
;
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
)
.

5.5.
Congruences

Dually to behavioural conformances on coalgebras, notions of congruence for algebras are modelled by liftings of their syntax functor:

Definition 5.24 (Congruence).

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
. A
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence
on a
\Sigma
-algebra
(A,a)
is a conformance
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
such that the following equivalent conditions hold:

a\colon(\Sigma A,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\boldsymbol{\cdot}$}}\;}}$}}(A,P)\;\;\iff\;\;\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P\sqsubseteq a^{\star}P\;\;\iff\;\;a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P\sqsubseteq P.

Example 5.25.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature with its associated polynomial functor
\Sigma\colon\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
. Depending on the choice of fibration and lifting, we recover various notions of congruence on
\Sigma
-algebras:

(1)

For the fibration
p_{\mathbf{rp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}\to\mathbf{Set}
and the lifting

\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(X,R)=\coprod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\underbrace{(X,R)\times\cdots\times(X,R)}_{\text{$\mathsf{ar}(\sigma)$ factors}},

a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on an algebra
A
is the usual notion from universal algebra: a relation
R\subseteq A\times A
such that if
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
is
n
-ary and
R(a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, then
R({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}))
.

(2)

For the fibration
p_{\mathbf{ne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}\to\mathbf{Set}
and the liftings

\widetilde{\Sigma}(X,d)=\coprod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\underbrace{(X,d)\times\cdots\times(X,d)}_{\text{$\mathsf{ar}(\sigma)$ factors}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(X,d)=\coprod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\underbrace{(X,d)\mathbin{\overline{\times}}\cdots\mathbin{\overline{\times}}(X,d)}_{\text{$\mathsf{ar}(\sigma)$ factors}},

a
\widetilde{\Sigma}
-congruence on an algebra
A
is fuzzy relation
d\colon A\times A\to[0,1]
satisfying the inequality
d({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}))\leq\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}d(a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime})
for all
a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}
and
n
-ary
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
. A
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence requires the weaker property
d({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}^{A}(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime})
instead.

The next lemma gives some properties of congruences; recall that
(-)^{+}
is the transitive closure.

Lemma 5.26.

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and let
(A,a)
be a
\Sigma
-algebra.

(1)

If
P
,
Q
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruences on
(A,a)
, then so is
P\cdot Q
.

(2)

If
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{A}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{\Sigma A}
preserves directed joins and
P
is a reflexive
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on
(A,a)
, then so is
P^{+}
.

In order to establish congruence properties of behavioural (bi)conformances on models of AHOS, we will make use of a fibrational generalization of
Howe’s method

(
Howe 1989
;
Howe 1996
)
. Its key ingredient is a purely algebraic concept, the
Howe closure
of a conformance:

Definition 5.27 (Howe Closure).

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and let
(A,a)
be a
\Sigma
-algebra. The
Howe closure
of a conformance
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
w.r.t. the algebra
(A,a)
is the
\sqsubseteq
-least conformance
\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
such that

\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}=P\sqcup(a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})\cdot P,

i.e. the least fixed point of the monotone endomap
Q\mapsto P\sqcup(a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma Q)\cdot P
on the complete lattice
{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
.

The key properties of the Howe closure are the following:

Lemma 5.28.

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, let
(A,a)
be a
\Sigma
-algebra, and let

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

be the Howe closure of
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
w.r.t.
(A,a)
.

(1)

If
P
is reflexive, then

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is reflexive and a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on
(A,a)
.

(2)

If
P
is transitive, then
\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}\cdot P\sqsubseteq\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}
.

(3)

Suppose that
p
is an
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal, and
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{A}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{\Sigma A}
preserves directed joins. If
P
is reflexive and symmetric, then
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})^{+}
is symmetric.

6.
Congruence Theorems for Abstract Higher-Order Specifications

We now put the categorical machinery of
Sections

4
and
5
together and develop a theory of congruence for behavioural (bi)conformances on canonical models of AHOS, which generalizes corresponding results for applicative (bi)simulations and their quantitative extensions and brings them onto a common foundation. The data for our congruence theorems is given by a complete AHOS (specifiying the syntax and operational semantics of the higher-order language at hand) together with a choice of liftings of its syntax and behaviour functors along a fibration, specifying the targeted forms of congruence and behavioural (bi)conformance.

Definition 6.1 (Lifting Situation).

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
be a complete AHOS. A
lifting situation
for
{\mathcal{S}}
is a triple
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
given by a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}^{(2)}
and liftings
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
of the functors
\Sigma^{(2)}
,
B^{(2)}
(cf.
Section

2
,
Notation
) along the fibration
p
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{(2)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}^{(2)})^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\mathsf{op}}\times p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle B$}}}}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{(2)}}

In our applications (
Section

7
), the fibration
p
is chosen as
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
(corresponding to bisimulations) or
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
(corresponding to behavioural distances). The requirement for the base category to be
\mathbb{B}^{(2)}
instead of
\mathbb{B}
is necessary for our approach to Howe’s method.

Notation 6.2.

We let
p_{0}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\to\mathbb{B}
denote the fibration arising via the change of base shown in the first diagram below. More explicitly,
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}
is the non-full subcategory of
{\mathbb{E}}
that has the same objects as
{\mathbb{E}}
, and all morphisms of
{\mathbb{E}}
that lie above some
\mathbb{B}^{(2)}
-morphism of the form
(f,f)
. The functor
p_{0}
is a restriction of
p
. Note that
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
restrict to liftings of
\Sigma
and
B
along
p_{0}
. We denote the restricted liftings by the same letters
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
, as shown in the second and third diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}^{\mathsf{op}}\times p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle B$}}}}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

For example, for the fibrations
p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
and
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
, the change of base yields the respective restrictions
p_{\mathbf{rp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{rp}}\to\mathbf{Set}
and
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}
(
5.5
(2)
).

In the following we isolate the two core conditions on a lifting situation that make our abstract congruence results work. The first condition states that the DCPO structure on
\mathbb{B}(\Lambda,B(\Lambda,\Lambda))
, which is used to construct the canonical model, interacts well with the order on fibers and reindexing:

Definition 6.3 (Compatibility).

A complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
is
compatible
with the lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
if for every
\leq
-directed family
c_{i}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
(
i\in I
) and every
d\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
, one has

(\bigvee_{i\in I}c_{i},d)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(P,Q)=\bigsqcap_{i\in I}\,(c_{i},d)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(P,Q)\qquad\text{for all $P,Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{\Lambda}$}.

The second condition says that the rule morphism should lift along the given fibration:

Definition 6.4 (Liftability).

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
be an AHOS and let
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
be a lifting situation for
{\mathcal{S}}
. We say that the rule morphism
\varrho
is
liftable w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}
if

\varrho\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B^{\infty}(P,P)\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\boldsymbol{\cdot}$}}\;}}$}}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(P,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}P)\qquad\text{for every $P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{\Lambda}$.}

(Note that
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B^{\infty}(P,P)
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(P,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}P)
are above
\Sigma B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
and
B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)
, resp., by
Proposition

5.16
.)

Liftability turns out to be the key to our congruence results below. Informally, it corresponds to a form of ‘continuity’ requirement on the operational rules represented by
\varrho
: whichever conformance, e.g. (fuzzy) relation,
P
is put above
\Lambda
, if the premises of a rule are related according to
P
, then the conclusions should be related according to
P
as well. In particular, since
P
is arbitrary, this requires that the operational rules should be parametrically polymorphic, i.e. they treat the program terms appearing in them as black-box variables and do not inspect their content. We will support these intuitions in our analysis of
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
in
Section

7
.

We are prepared to state the first half of our main result, which establishes congruence of the greatest behavioural conformance on the canonical model of a complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
w.r.t. a lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
. We regard the canonical model
\gamma\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
as a coalgebra for the endofunctor
B(\Lambda,-)\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
, which has a lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
along
p_{0}
(
6.2
). The greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
is denoted by
P_{\gamma}^{\to}
as in
5.18
.

Theorem 6.5 (Congruence of Behavioural Conformances).

Suppose that the following is given:

•

a complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
with its canonical model
(\Lambda,\gamma)
;

•

a compatible lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
, where
p
is a heterogeneous
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration and the liftings
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
are laxly monoidal.

If the rule morphism
\varrho
is liftable w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}
, then
P_{\gamma}^{\to}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on the initial algebra
(\Lambda,\iota)
.

The data of the congruence theorem is summarized by the three commutative diagrams below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\Lambda,\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\gamma$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}}}$}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B({\mathsf{id}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\iota$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}$}})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{(2)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{\mathsf{op}}\times{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{B}^{(2)})^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle B$}}}}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle B$}}}}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{(2)}}

Our proof of
Theorem

6.5
rests on a fibrational interpretation of Howe’s method
(
Howe 1989
;
Howe 1996
)
, which makes use of the abstract version of
Howe closure
introduced in
Definition

5.27
. The heart of the proof lies in the next proposition, which says that Howe closures preserve behavioural conformances:

Proposition 6.6 (Howe Closures of Behavioural Conformances).

In the setting of
Theorem

6.5
, suppose that
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{\Lambda}
is a reflexive and transitive
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
. Then the Howe closure

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

w.r.t. the initial algebra
(\Lambda,\iota)
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
.

Using this proposition, the congruence theorem now easily follows:

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

Let
P=P_{\gamma}^{\to}
be the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
. It is reflexive and transitive by
Lemma

5.28
. Thus, by
Proposition

6.6
, the Howe closure

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
. It follows that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}=P
;
indeed,
P\sqsubseteq\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}
holds by definition of

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

, and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}\sqsubseteq P
holds because
P
is the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
.
Since

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on
(\Lambda,\iota)
by
Lemma

5.28
(1)
, we conclude that
P
is a
\bar{\Sigma}
-congruence on
(\Lambda,\iota)
.
∎

Under slightly stronger assumptions on the fibration and the lifted functors, the congruence theorem extends from behavioural conformances to
bi
conformances. Following
5.21
, we let
P_{\gamma}^{\leftrightarrow}
denote the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural biconformance on the canonical model
(\Lambda,\gamma)
.

Theorem 6.7 (Congruence of Behavioural Biconformances).

Suppose that the following is given:

•

a complete AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{B},\Sigma,B,\leq,\varrho)
with its canonical model
(\Lambda,\gamma)
;

•

a compatible lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
, where
p
is a heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
is laxly monoidal, and
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{\Lambda}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{\Sigma\Lambda}
preserves directed joins.

If the rule morphism
\varrho
is liftable w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}
, then
P_{\gamma}^{\leftrightarrow}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on the initial algebra
(\Lambda,\iota)
.

The proof uses a fibrational generalization of Howe’s
transitive closure trick

(
Howe 1996
;
Pitts 2011
)
, which allows to reduce the congruence of behavioural biconformances to that of behavioural conformances.

Proof of
Theorem

6.7
.

Let
P=P^{\leftrightarrow}_{\gamma}
be the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural biconformance on
(\Lambda,\gamma)
. It is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive by
Lemma

5.22
. By
Proposition

6.6
, the Howe closure

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

w.r.t.
(\Lambda,\iota)
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural conformance. Then also the transitive closure
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})^{+}
of

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
behavioural conformance (
Lemma

5.19
), and it is symmetric by
Lemma

5.28
(3)
. In particular,
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})^{+}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural biconformance, and so

(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})^{+}=P

because
P
is the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-behavioural biconformance. Since

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence (
Lemma

5.28
), its transitive closure
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})^{+}
is also a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence (
Lemma

5.26
(2)
), and so
P
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence.
∎

Remark 6.8.

We have stated
Theorems

6.5
and
6.7
for general AHOS, whose rule morphisms assume existence of a cofree coalgebra
B^{\infty}(\Lambda,\Lambda)
. However, both theorems and their proofs can be straightforwardly restricted to depth-
n
AHOS, where a cofree coalgebra is not required.

Our general congruence theorems for AHOS (
Theorems

6.5
and
6.7
) reduce congruence proofs for higher-order languages to the systematic verification of a number of elementary conditions on the underlying fibrations, liftings, and rules. In comparison to usual ad-hoc congruence proofs, this can greatly simplify the derivation of congruence results for concrete languages, since the generic, boiler-plate aspects and much of the complexity of such proofs are hidden from the user of our theorems. We will demonstrate this point in the next section.

7.
Applications

We now put our abstract theory to use derive congruence results for the probabilistic higher-order languages
\mathbf{pSKI}
and
\mathbf{pBCKI}
by simple instantiation of
Theorem

6.7
.

7.1.
\mathbf{pBCKI}

As our first showcase, we study the behavioural pseudometric for the language
\mathbf{pBCKI}
, which corresponds to an affine probabilistic untyped
\lambda
-calculus. For that purpose, we instantiate
Theorem

6.7
to the AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}=(\mathbf{Set},\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}},B,\varrho_{\mathbf{pBCKI}})
for
\mathbf{pBCKI}
(
Example

4.7
)
and the lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
defined as follows:

(1)

p=p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
is the heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration of
Example

5.4
(3)
.

(2)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
of
\Sigma^{(2)}
is given as in
Example

5.25
(2)
; that is, we lift products via the non-standard lifting
\overline{\times}
of
Example

5.13
(3)
that computes sums of distances.

(3)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}
of
B^{(2)}
is given by the composite

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B=(\,\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$(-)_{\bot}$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\,),

where
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H
is the lifting of
H(X,Y)=Y^{X}
of
Example

5.13
(4)
,
(-)_{\bot}
is the lifting of the functor
X\mapsto\{\bot\}+X
of
Example

5.13
(1)
, and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}
is the lifting of the distribution functor
{\mathcal{D}}^{(2)}
of
Example

5.13
(5)
.

For brevity, we drop subscripts and put
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
,
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
and
\varrho=\varrho_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
. Moreover, we let
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
denote the behavioural pseudometric on the operational model
\gamma=\gamma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
, i.e. the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-fuzzy bisimulation (
Example

5.23
(2)
). Our abstract congruence result for behavioural biconformances (
Theorem

6.7
) then instantiates as follows:

Theorem 7.1 (Congruence of
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
).

For all
n
-ary operations
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
and
t_{i},s_{i}\in\Lambda
,

d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}))\leq d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t_{1},s_{1})+\cdots+d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t_{n},s_{n}).

For the proof, all we need to do is to systematically check that the conditions of
Theorem

6.7
hold for
{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{L}
. This is done in the next two lemmas. Let us start with the required properties of the lifted functors, which follow via a routine verification:

Lemma 7.2.

The lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
is compatible with
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
is laxly monoidal, and the map
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},\Lambda}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},\Sigma\Lambda}
preserves directed joins.

The key condition of
Theorem

6.7
, liftability of
\varrho
w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}
, corresponds to the following statement:

Lemma 7.3.

For every fuzzy relation
d
on
\Lambda
, the rule map (
7.1
) is non-expansive:

(7.1)

\varrho=\varrho_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma((\Lambda,d)\times\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d))\times\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d))))\to\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,d)).

Proof.

For ease of notation, we use the letter
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d
to denote all the fuzzy relations induced by
d
via the various liftings, e.g. the fuzzy relations on

(\Lambda,d)^{(\Lambda,d)},\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d)),\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d))),\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,d).

For example, we put
(\Lambda,d)^{(\Lambda,d)}=(\Lambda^{\Lambda},\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d)
etc. Note that
\times
in the domain of
\varrho
is the categorical product in
\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{ne}}
given by the maximum of distances, while the products appearing in the polynomial functor
\Sigma
are lifted via the sum of distances. Thus, non-expansivity of (
7.1
) means that for every
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
in
\Sigma
, every
(t_{i},\varphi_{i},\Phi_{i}),(t_{i}^{\prime},\varphi_{i}^{\prime},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\in\Lambda\times{B}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\times{B}(\Lambda,{B}(\Lambda,\Lambda))
and every
\varepsilon_{i}\in[0,1]
(
i=1,\ldots,n
),

\displaystyle d(t_{i},t_{i}),\,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}^{\prime}),\,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\Phi_{i},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{i}\quad(i=1,\ldots,n)

\displaystyle\implies~

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},\varphi_{1},\Phi_{1}),\ldots,(t_{n},\varphi_{n},\Phi_{n}))),\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1}^{\prime},\varphi_{1}^{\prime},\Phi_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(t_{n}^{\prime},\varphi_{n}^{\prime},\Phi_{n}^{\prime}))))\leq\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}.

We will now verify this property for every operator
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
.

\Omega
We have
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varrho(\Omega),\varrho(\Omega))=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(1\cdot\bot,1\cdot\bot)=0
, using that
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\bot,\bot)=0
.

B,B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime},C,C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime},K,K^{\prime},I
On each of these operations
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
, the map
\varrho
is defined as

\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n},-,-)))=1\cdot(t\mapsto T)

where
T=T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\in\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda
is a term in the variables
t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\Lambda
and each of the variables appears at most once in
T
, that is,
T
is an
affine
term. Now let
t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and suppose that
d(t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. By affinity of
T
and the definition of the fuzzy relation
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d
on the term algebra
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,d)
,

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(T[t,t_{1},\dots,t_{n}],T[t^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}])\leq d(t,t^{\prime})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\qquad\text{for all $t,t^{\prime}\in\Lambda$},

where
T[t^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,t_{n}^{\prime}]
is obtained from
T
by substituting the variables
t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
for
t^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}
.
This means that the functions
t\mapsto T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
and
t\mapsto T[t,t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}]
have distance at most
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}
in the hom-set fuzzy relation from
Example

5.13
(4)
, which proves that

\displaystyle\,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n},-,-)),\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1}^{\prime},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n}^{\prime},-,-))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(1\cdot(t\mapsto T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]),1\cdot(t\mapsto T[t,t_{1}^{\prime}\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}]))\leq\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}.

\oplus
For
k=1,2
let
\varphi_{k},\varphi_{k}^{\prime}\in B(\Lambda,\Lambda)={\mathcal{D}}(\{\top\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})
, and suppose
that
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{k},\varphi_{k}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle~\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varrho((-,\varphi_{1},-)\oplus(-,\varphi_{2},-)),\varrho((-,\varphi_{1}^{\prime},-)\oplus(-,\varphi_{2}^{\prime},-)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi_{2},\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varphi_{2}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{1}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{2},\varphi_{2}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}

where the inequality in the second step follows from
Lemma

5.14
(1)
.

\mathsf{app}
Let
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+({\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda}))^{\Lambda})
and
s,s^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and suppose that
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{1}
and
d(s,s^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{2}
. Using the map
j
from
4.9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\,((-,-,\Phi),(s,-,-)),\,((-,-,\Phi^{\prime}),(s^{\prime},-,-))=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(j(\Phi,s),j(\Phi^{\prime},s^{\prime}))\leq\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})+d(s,s^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}.

In the second step we use that

j\colon B((\Lambda,d),B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d)))\,\overline{\times}\,(\Lambda,d)\to B((\Lambda,d),(\Lambda,d))
is non-expansive. Indeed, all the maps appearing in the definition of
j
are non-expansive: for
\mathsf{st}
,
\eta
,
\mu
, this follows from
Lemma

5.14
, for
{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}}
from
Example

5.13
(4)
, and for
{\mathsf{outl}}
,
{\mathsf{inl}}
,
\nabla
it is clear. ∎

The congruence property of
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
has some useful consequences. In particular, it entails that
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
relates well to the
contextual pseudometric

d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
(
Section

3
). Let us first note that the latter has a convenient alternative characterization. We say that a pseudometric
d
on
\Lambda
is
adequate (for termination)
if

d(t,s)\geq|\gamma(t)(\bot)-\gamma(s)(\bot)|
for all
t,s\in\Lambda
. Then the following holds:

Proposition 7.4.

The pseudometric
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is the
\sqsubseteq
-greatest adequate
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on
(\Lambda,\iota)
.

It follows that
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
yields a sound method for proving upper bounds to contextual distances:

Corollary 7.5 (Soundness of
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
).

For all
t,s\in\Lambda
one has
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t,s)\leq d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t,s)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

7.4
and since the pseudometric
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence (
Theorem

7.1
), we only need to prove that
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is adequate. This follows from the observation that given
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\cdots)
, every transportation plan from
\varphi
to
\psi
must send at least
|\varphi(\bot)-\psi(\bot)|
units from locations distinct from
\bot
to the location
\bot
, which incurs a cost of at least
|\varphi(\bot)-\psi(\bot)|
.
∎

Example 7.6.

We prove
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(I,I\oplus\Omega)=\frac{1}{2}
. For ‘
\geq
’, consider the empty context. For ‘
\leq
’, put
d=d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
. By
Corollary

7.5
it suffices to show
d(I,I\oplus\Omega)\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Since
d
is a fuzzy simulation, for that it is enough to show that
\gamma(I)=1\cdot(t\mapsto t)
and
\gamma(I\oplus\Omega)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bot+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(t\mapsto t)
have distance at most
\frac{1}{2}
w.r.t. the fuzzy relation on
{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+\Lambda^{\Lambda})
induced by
d
. This holds as
d
is reflexive.

7.2.
\mathbf{pSKI}

As our second application, we consider the language
\mathbf{pSKI}
, which corresponds to a (non-affine) probabilistic untyped
\lambda
-calculus. Unlike
d^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
, it turns out the behavioural pseudometric
d^{\mathbf{pSKI}}
is not a congruence. This is due to the fact that non-affine contexts can amplify distances, leading to trivialization of the contextual pseudometric
(
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
)
. In our categorical setup, the failure of congruence is conceptually reflected by the fact that the rule map
\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
of
\mathbf{pSKI}
does not lift along the fibration
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}
. Indeed, the argument for the combinator case in
Lemma

7.3
uses that the behaviour of all combinators is given by affine terms, which is not true for the combinator
S
.

However,
\mathbf{pSKI}
still has a well-behaved qualitative notion of bisimulation, which emerges in our setting by restricting fuzzy relations to relations. Thus we instantiate
Theorem

6.7
to the AHOS
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pSKI}}=(\mathbf{Set},\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}},B,\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}})

and the lifting situation
