\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
defined as follows:

(1)

p=p_{\mathbf{jrp}}\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
is the heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration of
Example

5.4
(1)
.

(2)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of
\Sigma^{(2)}\colon\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
is given by
Example

5.25
(1)
.

(3)

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B\colon\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\to\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
of
B^{(2)}
is given by the composite

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B=(\,\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$(-)_{\bot}$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.75pt}{$\xrightarrow{\;\smash{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{{\scriptsize$\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}$}}\;}}$}}\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}\,),

using the liftings
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H
,
(-)_{\bot}
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}
as defined in
Example

5.15
.

The derivation of the congruence result is now much analogous to the case of
\mathbf{pBCKI}
. In the following we put
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
,
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
and
\varrho=\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
. Moreover, we let
\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}
denote the bisimilarity relation on the canonical model
\gamma=\gamma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
, that is, the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(1_{\Lambda},-)
-bisimulation (
Example

5.23
(1)
). Our abstract congruence result for behavioural biconformances (
Theorem

6.7
) then instantiates to:

Theorem 7.7 (Congruence of
\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}
).

For all
n
-ary operations
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
and
t_{i},s_{i}\in\Lambda
,

t_{1}\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge t_{n}\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}s_{n}\implies{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}).

Again, we only need to check the conditions of
Theorem

6.7
.

Lemma 7.8.

The lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
is compatible with
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal, the lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
is laxly monoidal, and the map
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},\Lambda}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},\Sigma\Lambda}
preserves directed joins.

This is immediate from the corresponding statement for
\mathbf{pBCKI}
(
Lemma

7.2
) since the lifting situation for
\mathbf{pSKI}
restricts that for
\mathbf{pBCKI}
. Liftability of
\varrho
w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}
amounts to the following:

Lemma 7.9.

For every relation
R
on
\Lambda
, the rule map (
7.2
) is relation-preserving:

(7.2)

\varrho=\varrho_{\mathbf{pSKI}}\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma((\Lambda,R)\times\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),(\Lambda,R))\times\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),(\Lambda,R))))\to\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,R)).

The proof is identical to that of
Lemma

7.3
, restricted to relations (i.e.
\{0,1\}
-valued fuzzy relations). We only need to observe that in the proof case for combinators, the affinity of the term
T
is not needed if
d
is
\{0,1\}
-valued. Therefore, for such
d
the argument also applies to
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}
.

Analogously to
Corollary

7.5
, we get soundness of bisimulations for
contextual equivalence
:

Corollary 7.10 (Soundness of
\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}
).

For all
t,s\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{pSKI}}
, if
t\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}s
then
t\approx^{\mathbf{pSKI}}_{\mathrm{ctx}}s
.

The reader should have noted that our proofs of
Theorems

7.1
and
7.7
are fairly short, modular, and almost mechanical. This simplicity is enabled by the clean separation and isolation of required conditions on the lifted functors and the operational rules of the language in our abstract congruence results (
Theorems

6.5
and
6.7
). In particular, we mention that our above proofs are markedly simpler than existing congruence proofs for probabilistic
\lambda
-calculi, which rest on non-trivial calculations based on linear programming duality
(
Crubillé and Dal Lago 2015
)
or combinatorial properties of sets of real numbers
(
Lago et al. 2014
)
.

8.
Conclusion and Future Work

We have introduced
abstract higher-order specifications
(
AHOS
), a categorical framework for specifying operational semantics of higher-order languages that combines the strengths of
higher-order abstract GSOS
and
monotone biGSOS
and does away with some of their limitations. Our main application of the new framework is a theory of congruence of behavioural conformances, parametric in a choice of a fibration and based on a fibrational generalization of Howe’s method.

While we have put the focus of this paper on the foundational aspects of the AHOS framework and necessarily restricted ourselves to relatively basic instantiations, we expect that the generality and the strikingly simple categorical setup will make the framework widely applicable. In particular, we strongly expect that all language features that have previously been modelled in the higher-order abstract GSOS framework, such as variables and binding
(
Goncharov et al. 2023
)
, call-by-value and call-by-push-value evaluation
(
Goncharov et al. 2025c
)
, simple
(
Goncharov et al. 2024b
)
and recursive
(
Goncharov et al. 2024a
)
types, and higher-order store
(
Goncharov et al. 2025a
)
, carry over without complications to the AHOS framework by instantiating it over suitable presheaf categories.

As a challenging application of our results, we aim to study probabilistic higher-order languages over
continuous
distributions in the AHOS framework, aiming for a theory of behavioural distance for such languages. So far, only a qualitative notion of bisimilarity has emerged in the literature
(
Lago and Gavazzo 2019
)
.

A further natural step is to develop a fibrational theory of
logical relations
, which besides coinduction are the most popular operational technique for establishing contextual equivalence. We expect that the recent work on fibrational differential logical relations
(
Dagnino and Gavazzo 2024
)
will provide inspiration. If logical relations and their congruence properties can be developed at the generality of AHOS, they may apply to relational reasoning far beyond contextual equivalence, e.g. to
parametricity

(
Reynolds 1983
)
.

Appendix A
Complete Lattice Fibrations and Topological Functors

In this appendix we give a proof of the folklore result that
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibrations are exactly fiber-small topological functors (
Theorem

A.5
). Let us first recall the notion of topological functor
(
Adámek et al. 1990
)
:

Definition A.1 (Topological functor).

Let
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
be a functor.

(1)

A
p
-source
is a possible large family of morphisms
(f_{i}\colon X\to pY_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathbb{B}
.

(2)

An
initial lift
of
(f_{i})
is a source
(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f_{i}\colon\overline{X}\to Y_{i})_{i\in I}
in
{\mathbb{E}}
such that
p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f_{i}=f_{i}
for each
i\in I
, and moreover for every source
(g_{i}\colon Z\to Y_{i})
and
m\colon pZ\to X
such that
pg_{i}=f_{i}\circ m
for all
i\in I
, there exists a unique
\overline{m}\colon Z\to Y
such that
p\overline{m}=m
and
g_{i}=\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$f$}}}}f_{i}\circ\overline{m}
for all
i\in I
. The dual notion is that of a
final lift
of a
p
-sink

(f_{i}\colon pX_{i}\to Y)_{i\in I}
.

(3)

p
is a
topological functor
if every
p
-source has a unique initial lift.

(4)

A topological functor is
fiber-small
if each fiber
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
,
X\in\mathbb{B}
, is a small category.

Lemma A.2 (
(
Adámek et al. 1990
)
, Exercise 21C).

A functor
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is topological iff the following conditions hold:

(1)

Each fiber is a (possibly large) complete lattice.

(2)

Each morphism
f\colon X\to pY
(viewed as a singleton
p
-source) has a unique initial lift.

(3)

Each morphism
f\colon pX\to Y
(viewed as singleton
p
-sink) has a unique final lift.

Recall the definition of a fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
(
5.1
). We say that
p
is a
poset fibration
if each fiber
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
(
X\in B
) is a small poset. Thus every
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration is a poset fibration.

Lemma A.3.

In a poset fibration, cartesian lifts are unique.

Proof.

By its universal property, the cartesian lift of a morphism
f\colon X\to pY
is unique up to isomorphism in the fiber
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
. Hence it is unique as isomorphisms in the poset
{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
are identities.
∎

Lemma A.4 (
(
Jacobs 1999
)
, Lemma 9.1.2).

A fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is a bifibration iff for each
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
the reindexing functor
f^{\star}\colon{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to{\mathbb{E}}_{X}
has a left adjoint.

The desired correspondence result is now a straightforward consequence of the above lemmas:

Theorem A.5.

A functor
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration iff it is topological and fiber-small.

Proof.

(
\mathrel{\Rightarrow}
) Suppose that
p
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration. Since each reindexing functor
f^{\star}
preserves meets, it has a left adjoint by the adjoint functor theorem, so
p
is a bifibration (
Lemma

A.4
). To prove that
p
is topological, we verify the conditions of
Lemma

A.2
. Condition (1) holds by hypothesis. Condition (2) follows from
p
being a fibration and by
Lemma

A.3
; note that an initial lift of a morphism is the same as a cartesian lift. Dually, condition (3) follows from
p
being an opfibration.

(
\Leftarrow
) Suppose that
p
is a fiber-small topological functor. By condition (1) of
Lemma

A.2
, each fiber is a complete lattice. By conditions (2) and (3) of
Lemma

A.2
, the functor
p
is a bifibration; in particular, it is a fibration, and each reindexing functor
f^{\star}
has a left adjoint (
Lemma

A.4
), which implies that it preserves meets. Therefore,
p
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration.
∎

Appendix B
Omitted Proofs

Proof of
Lemma

5.2

The four statements of the lemma are immediate from the fact every
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
is a topological functor (
Theorem

A.5
).

(1)

p
is faithful by
(
Adámek et al. 1990
, Thm. 21.3)
.

(2)

p
is a bifibration by
Lemma

A.2
.

(3)

There is a Galois connection
f_{\star}\dashv f^{\star}
by
Lemma

A.4
.

(4)

Since
f^{\star}\circ g^{\star}
is a cartesian lift of
gf
, we have
f^{\star}\circ g^{\star}=(gf)^{\star}
by the uniqueness of cartesian lifts (
Lemma

A.3
). The other statement is dual.

Details for
Example

5.9

We prove that
p_{\mathbf{jne}}\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{\mathbf{jne}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{(2)}
is a heterogeneous
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration (the proof for
\mathbf{Rel}_{\mathbf{jrp}}
is analogous).
Let us verify the three axioms (
5.12
).

For the first axiom of (
5.12
), let
d_{=}
denote the fuzzy relation corresponding to the identity relation, i.e.
d_{=}(x,x^{\prime})=0
if
x=x^{\prime}
and
d_{=}(x,x^{\prime})=1
otherwise. Then

(f,f)^{\star}d_{=}(x,x^{\prime})=d_{=}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq d_{=}(x,x^{\prime}).

For the last inequality one distinguishes two cases: for
x\neq x^{\prime}
it holds trivially since
d_{=}(x,x^{\prime})=1
, and for
x=x^{\prime}
it holds because both sides are equal to
0
.

For the second axiom of (
5.12
), let
d,e\colon Y\times Y\to[0,1]
be fuzzy relations. Then

\displaystyle(f,h)^{\star}(d\cdot e)(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(d\cdot e)(f(x),h(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in Y}d(f(x),y)+e(y,h(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{x^{\prime\prime}\in X}d(f(x),g(x^{\prime\prime}))+e(g(x^{\prime\prime}),h(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{x^{\prime\prime}\in X}(f,g)^{\star}d(x,x^{\prime\prime})+(g,h)^{\star}e(x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=((f,g)^{\star}d\cdot(g,h)^{\star}e)(x,x^{\prime}).

The third axiom of (
5.12
) actually holds with equality: for every
d\colon Y\times Y\to[0,1]
, we have

((f,f)^{\star}d)^{\circ}(x,x^{\prime})=((f,f)^{\star}d)(x^{\prime},x)=d(f(x^{\prime}),f(x))=d^{\circ}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))=((f,f)^{\star}d^{\circ})(x,x^{\prime}).

Details for
Example

5.13
(4)

We prove that
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H
is properly defined on morphisms, that is, for any two pairs of jointly non-expansive maps
(f,g)\colon(\overline{X},d_{\overline{X}})\to(X,d_{X})
and
(h,k)\colon(Y,d_{Y})\to(\overline{Y},d_{\overline{Y}})
, the pair
H((f,g),(h,k))
is jointly non-expansive from
(Y^{X},d_{Y^{X}})
to
(\overline{Y}^{\overline{X}},d_{\overline{Y}^{\overline{X}}})
:

(B.1)

d_{\overline{Y}^{\overline{X}}}(h\circ u\circ f,k\circ v\circ g)\leq d_{Y^{X}}(u,v)\qquad\text{for all $u,v\in Y^{X}$}.

To see this, we compute for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\overline{X}
:

\displaystyle d_{\overline{Y}}(h\circ u\circ f(x),k\circ v\circ g(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{Y}(u\circ f(x),v\circ g(x^{\prime}))

(h,k)
jointly non-exp.

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))+d_{Y^{X}}(u,v)

def.
d_{Y^{X}}

\displaystyle\leq d_{\overline{X}}(x,x^{\prime})+d_{Y^{X}}(u,v)

(f,g)
jointly non-exp.

which proves (
B.1
) by definition of
d_{\overline{Y}^{\overline{X}}}
.

Details for
Example

5.13
(5)

Let us check that
{\mathcal{D}}
is properly defined on morphisms, that is, for every jointly non-expansive pair
(f,g)\colon(X,d_{X})\to(Y,d_{Y})
the pair
({\mathcal{D}}f,{\mathcal{D}}g)\colon({\mathcal{D}}X,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{X})\to({\mathcal{D}}Y,\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{Y})
is jointly non-expansive:

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{Y}({\mathcal{D}}f(\varphi),{\mathcal{D}}g(\varphi^{\prime}))\leq\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{X}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\qquad\text{for all $\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}X$}.

This follows from the observation that every solution
(t_{i,j})
for the transportation from
\varphi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and
\varphi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{D}}X
is also a solution for the transportation problem from
{\mathcal{D}}f(\varphi)=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot f(x_{i})
and
{\mathcal{D}}g(\varphi^{\prime})=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot g(x_{j}^{\prime})
, and moreover the cost of the latter is

\sum_{i,j}d_{Y}(f(x_{i}),g(x_{j}^{\prime}))\cdot t_{i,j}\leq\sum_{i,j}d_{X}(x_{i},x_{j^{\prime}})\cdot t_{i,j},

i.e. it is bounded above by the cost the solution for the original transportation problem.

Proof of
Lemma

5.14

Given a fuzzy relation
(X,d)
we put
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d=\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d
.

(1)

Let
p\in[0,1]
and let
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}X
be distributions

\varphi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i},\quad\varphi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime},\quad\psi=\sum_{i\in I}q_{i}\cdot x_{i},\quad\psi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}q_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime},

so that

p\cdot\varphi+(1-p)\cdot\psi=\sum_{i\in I}(p\cdot p_{i}+(1-p)\cdot q_{i})\cdot x_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad p\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot\psi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}(p\cdot p_{j}^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot q_{j}^{\prime})\cdot x_{j}^{\prime}.

Let
(s_{i,j})_{i,j}
and
(t_{i,j})_{i,j}
be optimal solutions of the transportation problem from
\varphi
to
\varphi^{\prime}
and from
\psi
to
\psi^{\prime}
, respectively. Thus

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}s_{i,j}=p_{i}\;(i\in I),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}s_{i,j}=p_{j}^{\prime}\;(j\in J),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}{d}(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot s_{i,j}=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}).

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}t_{i,j}=q_{i}\;(i\in I),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}t_{i,j}=q_{j}^{\prime}\;(j\in J),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}{d}(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot t_{i,j}=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\psi,\psi^{\prime}).

Then
(r_{i,j})_{i,j}:=(p\cdot s_{i,j}+(1-p)\cdot t_{i,j})_{i,j}
is a solution of the transportation problem from
p\cdot\varphi+(1-p)\cdot\psi
to
p\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot\psi^{\prime}
, that is,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}r_{i,j}=p\cdot p_{i}+(1-p)\cdot q_{i}\;(i\in I),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}r_{i,j}=p\cdot p_{j}^{\prime}+(1-p)\cdot q_{j}^{\prime}\;(j\in J).

Indeed, the first equation follows from

\sum_{j}r_{i,j}=\sum_{j}p\cdot s_{i,j}+(1-p)\cdot t_{i,j}=p\cdot\sum_{j}s_{i,j}+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{j}t_{i,j}=p\cdot p_{i}+(1-p)\cdot q_{i}.

The proof of the second equation is analogous. The cost of the solution
(r_{i,j})_{i,j}
is

\sum_{i,j}d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot r_{i,j}=p\cdot\sum_{i,j}d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot s_{i,j}+(1-p)\cdot\sum_{i,j}d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot t_{i,j}=p\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\psi)+(1-p)\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}).

Thus
(r_{i,j})_{i,j}
witnesses the inequality (
5.13
).

(2)

Let
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}X
and
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
, where

\varphi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i},\quad\varphi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j}^{\prime},\quad\mathsf{st}(\varphi,y)=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot(x_{i},y),\quad\mathsf{st}(\varphi^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot(x_{j}^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

Letting
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{+}
denote the Wasserstein metric on
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}((X,d)\overline{\times}\,(Y,d^{\prime}))
, we need to prove

(B.2)

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d_{+}(\mathsf{st}(\varphi,y),\mathsf{st}(\varphi^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\leq\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})+d^{\prime}(y,y^{\prime}).

Let
(t_{i,j})_{i,j}
be an optimal solution of the transportation problem from
\varphi
to
\varphi^{\prime}
; that is,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}t_{i,j}=p_{i}\;(i\in I),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}t_{i,j}=p_{j}^{\prime}\;(j\in J),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}{d}(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot t_{i,j}=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}).

Then
(t_{i,j})_{i,j}
is also a solution of the transportation problem from
\mathsf{st}(\varphi,y)
to
\mathsf{st}(\varphi^{\prime},y^{\prime})
of cost

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}d_{+}((x_{i},y),(x_{j}^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\cdot t_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}(d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})+d^{\prime}(y,y^{\prime}))\cdot t_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}d(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\cdot t_{i,j}+d^{\prime}(y,y^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{i,j}t_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})+d^{\prime}(y,y^{\prime}).

using that
\sum_{i,j}t_{i,j}=1
. This proves (
B.2
).

(3)

For
x,x^{\prime}\in X
the unique solution of the transportation problem from
\eta(x)=1\cdot x
to
\eta(x^{\prime})=1\cdot x^{\prime}
is given by sending all available supply from
x
to
x^{\prime}
; the cost of this solution is
d(x,x^{\prime})
. Therefore

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\eta(x),\eta(x^{\prime}))=d(x,x^{\prime}),

proving that
\eta
is non-expansive (in fact, isometric).

(4)

To show non-expansivity of
\mu
, let
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{D}}X
, say

\Phi=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot\varphi_{i}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\varphi_{i}=\sum_{k\in K_{i}}p_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k},

\Phi^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot\varphi_{j}^{\prime}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\varphi_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\in L_{j}}p_{j,l}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j,l}^{\prime}.

Letting
\tilde{d}
denote the Wasserstein distance on
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(X,d)
, we need to prove

(B.3)

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\mu(\Phi),\mu(\Phi^{\prime}))=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{k\in K_{i}}p_{i}\cdot p_{i,k}\cdot x_{i,k},\,\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{l\in L_{j}}p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot p_{j,l}^{\prime}\cdot x_{j,l}^{\prime})\leq\tilde{d}(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}).

Let
(s_{i,j})_{i,j}
be an optimal solution of the transportation problem from
\Phi
to
\Phi^{\prime}
; that is,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}s_{i,j}=p_{i}\;(i\in I),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}s_{i,j}=p_{j}^{\prime}\;(j\in J),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}^{\prime})\cdot s_{i,j}=\tilde{d}(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}).

Moreover, for each
i\in I
and
j\in J
let
(t_{k,l}^{i,j})_{k\in K_{i},l\in L_{j}}
be an optimal solution of the transportation problem from
\varphi_{i}
to
\varphi_{j}^{\prime}
; that is,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l\in L_{j}}t_{k,l}^{i,j}=p_{i,k}\;(k\in K_{i}),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\in K_{i}}t_{k,l}^{i,j}=p_{j,l}^{\prime}\;(l\in L_{j}),\qquad\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\in K_{i},l\in L_{j}}d(x_{i,k},x_{j,l}^{\prime})\cdot t_{k,l}^{i,j}=\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}^{\prime}).

Then
(r_{(i,k),(j,i)})_{(i,k),(j,l)}:=(s_{i,j}\cdot t_{k,l}^{i,j})_{(i,k),(j,l)}
is a solution to the transportation problem from
\mu(\Phi)
to
\mu(\Phi^{\prime})
; that is,

\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{l\in L_{j}}r_{(i,k),(j,l)}=p_{i}\cdot p_{i,k}\;(i\in I,\,k\in K_{i}),\qquad\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{k\in K_{i}}r_{(i,k),(j,l)}=p_{j}^{\prime}\cdot p_{j,l}^{\prime}\;(j\in J,\,l\in L_{j}).

Indeed, the first equation follows from the computation

\sum_{j}\sum_{l}r_{(i,k),(j,l)}=\sum_{j}\sum_{l}s_{i,j}\cdot t_{k,l}^{i,j}=\sum_{j}s_{i,j}\sum_{l}t_{k,l}^{i,j}=\sum_{j}s_{i,j}\cdot p_{i,k}=p_{i}\cdot p_{i,k};

analogously for the second equation. The cost of the solution
(r_{(i,k),(j,i)})_{(i,k),(j,l)}
is

\displaystyle\sum_{i,k,j,l}d(x_{i,k},x^{\prime}_{j,l})\cdot r_{(i,k),(j,l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,k,j,l}d(x_{i,k},x^{\prime}_{j,l})\cdot s_{i,j}\cdot t_{k,l}^{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}s_{i,j}\cdot\sum_{k,l}d(x_{i,k},x^{\prime}_{j,l})\cdot t_{k,l}^{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}s_{i,j}\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tilde{d}(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}).

This proves (
B.3
).

Proof of
Proposition

5.16

Let
F^{\star}X
be the free
F
-algebra on
X
, with structure and unit

\iota_{X}\colon FF^{\star}X\to F^{\star}X\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta_{X}\colon X\to F^{\star}X

For every
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra
(A,a)
, the pair
(pA,pa)
is an
F
-algebra. Therefore, for every
f\colon P\to A
in
{\mathbb{E}}
, the morphism
pf\colon X\to pA
uniquely extends to an
F
-algebra morphism

(pf)^{\#}\colon(F^{\star}X,\iota_{X})\to(pA,pa)\qquad\text{such that}\qquad pf=(pf)^{\#}\circ\eta_{X}.

We define the object
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{F^{\star}X}
by

\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P:=\bigsqcap_{(A,a)\in\Alg(\overline{F}),\,f\colon P\to A}((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A.

The proposition then immediately follows from the following statements:

(a)

There exists a (necessarily unique) morphism
\eta_{P}\colon P\to\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
above
\eta_{X}
.

(b)

There exists a (necessarily unique) morphism
\iota_{P}\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\to\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
above
\iota_{X}
.

(c)

(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P,\iota_{P})
is the free
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra with unit
\eta_{P}
: for every
(A,a)\in\Alg(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F)
and
f\colon P\to A
in
{\mathbb{E}}
, there exists unique
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra morphism
f^{\#}\colon(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P,\iota_{P})\to(A,a)
such that
f=f^{\#}\circ\eta_{P}
. Moreover, the morphism
f^{\#}
is above
(pf)^{\#}
.

Proof of
(a)
:
We need to prove
P\sqsubseteq\eta_{X}^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
. For every
(A,a)\in\Alg(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F)
and
f\colon P\to A
we have

P\sqsubseteq(pf)^{\star}A=((pf)^{\#}\circ\eta_{X})^{\star}A=\eta_{X}^{\star}((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A,

where the first step uses that
f\colon P\to A
in
{\mathbb{E}}
and the second step uses
Lemma

5.2
(4)
. Since
\eta_{X}^{\star}
preserves meets, it follows that

P\sqsubseteq\bigsqcap_{(A,a)\in\Alg(\overline{F}),\,f\colon P\to A}\eta_{X}^{\star}((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A=\eta_{X}^{\star}\bigsqcap_{(A,a)\in\Alg(\overline{F}),\,f\colon P\to A}((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A=\eta_{X}^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P.

Proof of
(b)
: We need to prove
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\sqsubseteq\iota_{X}^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P
. Fix
(A,a)\in\Alg(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F)
and
f\colon P\to A
, and
denote the cartesian lift of
(pf)^{\#}\colon F^{\star}X\to pA
by

\overline{(pf)^{\#}}\colon((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A\to A.

Then the diagram below commutes, where
i\colon\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\to((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A
is the unique morphism in
{\mathbb{E}}_{F^{\star}X}
witnessing that
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\sqsubseteq((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FF^{\star}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FpA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FF^{\star}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\star}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pA}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}Fi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\bar{F}\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fp\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(pf)^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(pf)^{\#}}

Indeed, the three upper parts commute because
Fp=p\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
, the middle left part commutes because
pi={\mathsf{id}}
and
\overline{(pf)^{\#}}
is above
(pf)^{\#}
by definition, and the lower part commutes because
(pf)^{\#}\colon(F^{\star}X,\iota_{X})\to(pA,pa)
is an
F
-algebra morphism. The outside of the above diagram thus shows that the composite
a\circ\bar{F}\overline{(pf)^{\#}}\circ\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}Fi
is above
(pf)^{\#}\circ\iota_{X}
. Therefore, the universal property of the cartesian lift
\overline{(pf)^{\#}}
implies that there exists a unique dashed morphism above
\iota_{X}
making the diagram below commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}Fi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{F}\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}

This proves
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\sqsubseteq\iota_{X}^{\star}((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A
for every
(A,a)\in\Alg(F)
and
f\colon P\to X
, whence
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P\sqsubseteq\iota_{X}^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P

because
\iota_{X}^{\star}
preserves meets.

Proof of
(c)
: Given
(A,a)\in\Alg(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F)
and
f\colon P\to A
, the morphism
f^{\#}:=\overline{(pf)^{\#}}\circ i
is an
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra morphism from
(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P,\dot{\iota}_{P})
to
(A,a)
satisfying
f=f^{\#}\circ\eta_{P}
, that is, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((pf)^{\#})^{\star}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}Fi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(pf)^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

Indeed, it suffices to show that the diagram commutes with the faithful functor
p
applied to it, and this yields precisely the commutative diagram stating that
(pf)^{\#}\colon(F^{\star}X,\iota_{X})\to(pA,pa)
is an
F
-algebra morphism satisfying
pf=(pf)^{\#}\circ\eta_{X}
.

To show uniqueness of
f^{\#}
, suppose that
h\colon(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P,\iota_{P})\to(A,a)
is an
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-algebra morphism satisfying
f=h\circ\eta_{P}
; that is, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F^{\star}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}Fh}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

Applying
p
to this diagram shows that
ph\colon(F^{\star}X,\iota_{X})\to(pA,pa)
is an
F
-algebra morphism satisfying
pf=ph\circ\eta_{X}
. Therefore
ph=(pf)^{\#}
, which uniquely determines
h
because
p
is faithful.

Proof of
Lemma

5.19

The statements of
Lemma

5.19
are subsumed by the following lemma, which establishes additional properties of behavioural conformances:

Lemma B.1.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a laxly monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
along the
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and suppose that
(C,c)
is an
F
-coalgebra.

(1)

The unit
1_{C}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on
(C,c)
.

(2)

If
P,Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances on
(C,c)
, then so is
P\cdot Q
.

(3)

If
P_{i}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
(
i\in I
) are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances on
(C,c)
, then so is
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}P_{i}
.

(4)

If
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance on
(C,c)
, then so is its transitive closure
P^{+}
.

(5)

The coalgebra
(C,c)
has a greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance
P_{c}^{\to}
, given by the join in
{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
of all
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances.

(6)

The greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance
P_{c}^{\to}
on
(C,c)
is reflexive and transitive.

Proof.

(1)

1_{C}
is
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance because

1_{C}\sqsubseteq c^{\star}1_{FC}\sqsubseteq c^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F1_{C};

the first step uses that
p
is a
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration and the second one that
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
is laxly monoidal.

(2)

Given
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances
P,Q
, the composite
P\cdot Q
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance because

P\cdot Q\sqsubseteq c^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP\cdot c^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FQ\sqsubseteq c^{\star}(\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FQ)\sqsubseteq c^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F(P\cdot Q);

the first step uses that
P
and
Q
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances, the second one that
p
is a
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration, and the third one that
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
is laxly monoidal.

(3)

If
P_{i}
(
i\in I
) are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances, where
I\neq\emptyset
, then
P=\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance because

c_{\star}P=c_{\star}\bigsqcup_{i\in I}P_{i}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}c_{\star}P_{i}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP_{i}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP\sqsubseteq\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}FP.

The second step uses that
p
is a
\mathbf{CLat}_{\sqcap}
-fibration (so
c_{\star}
preserves all joins), the third one that each
P_{i}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance, and the fourth one that
P_{i}\sqsubseteq P
and
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
is a functor.

(4)

If
P
is an
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance, then
P^{+}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}P^{n}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance by parts
(2)
and
(3)
.

(5)

Immediate from part
(3)
.

(6)

By parts
(1)
and
(2)
, we know that
1_{C}
and
P_{c}^{\to}\cdot P_{c}^{\to}
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformances on
(C,c)
. Since
P_{c}^{\to}
is the greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural conformance, it follows that
1_{C}\sqsubseteq P_{c}^{\to}
and
P_{c}^{\to}\cdot P_{c}^{\to}\sqsubseteq P_{c}^{\to}
, so
P_{c}^{\to}
is reflexive and transitive.∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.22

We need a simple auxiliary result:

Lemma B.2.

Let
M
be a quantale and
x\in M
.

(1)

The element
x^{+}
is the least transitive element of
M
such that
x\sqsubseteq x^{+}
.

(2)

If
M
is involutive, then
(x^{\circ})^{+}=(x^{+})^{\circ}
.

Proof.

(1)

The element
x^{+}
is transitive because

x^{+}\cdot x^{+}=(\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}x^{n})\cdot(\bigsqcup_{m\geq 1}x^{m})=\bigsqcup_{n,m\geq 1}x^{n+m}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}x^{n}=x^{+}.

Moreover, if
x\sqsubseteq y
and
y
is transitive, then
x^{n}\sqsubseteq y^{n}\sqsubseteq y
for all
n\geq 1
, hence
x^{+}\sqsubseteq y
.

(2)

This follows from the computation

(x^{\circ})^{+}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}(x^{\circ})^{n}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}(x^{n})^{\circ}=(\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}x^{n})^{\circ}=(x^{+})^{\circ},

where the second and the third step use that
M
is an involutive quantale.
∎

The statement of
Lemma

5.22
is subsumed by the following lemma, which establishes additional properties of behavioural biconformances:

Lemma B.3.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a laxly monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
along the
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
, and suppose that
(C,c)
is an
F
-coalgebra.

(1)

The unit
1_{C}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on
(C,c)
.

(2)

If
P,Q\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformances on
(C,c)
, then so is
P\cdot Q
.

(3)

If
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on
(C,c)
, then so is
P^{\circ}
.

(4)

If
P_{i}\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
(
i\in I
) are
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformances in
(C,c)
, then so is
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}P_{i}
.

(5)

If
P\in{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance on
(C,c)
, then so is its transitive closure
P^{+}
.

(6)

The coalgebra
(C,c)
has a greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance
P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
, given by the join in
{\mathbb{E}}_{C}
of all
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformances.

(7)

The greatest
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$F$}}}}F
-behavioural biconformance
P^{\leftrightarrow}_{c}
on
(C,c)
is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.

Proof.

(1)

This follows from
Lemma

B.1
(1)
since
1_{C}^{\circ}=1_{C}
.

(2)

Let
P,Q
be behavioural biconformances. Then
P,P^{\circ},Q,Q^{\circ}
are behavioural conformances, so
P\cdot Q
and
(P\cdot Q)^{\circ}=Q^{\circ}\cdot P^{\circ}
are behavioural conformances by
Lemma

B.1
(2)
. This shows that
P\cdot Q
is a behavioural biconformance.

(3)

Immediate from the definition of a behavioural biconformance, since
(P^{\circ})^{\circ}=P
.

(4)

Let
P_{i}
(
i\in I
) be behavioural biconformances, where
I\neq\emptyset
. Then
P_{i}
and
P_{i}^{\circ}
are behavioural conformances, so
\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i}
and
(\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i})^{\circ}=\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i}^{\circ}
are behavioural conformances by
Lemma

B.1
(3)
, so
\bigsqcup_{i}P_{i}
is a behavioural biconformance.

(5)

Let
P
be a behavioural biconformance. Then
P
and
P^{\circ}
are behavioural conformances, so
P^{+}
and
(P^{\circ})^{+}
are behavioural conformances by
Lemma

B.1
(4)
. Since

(P^{+})^{\circ}=(P^{\circ})^{+}
by
Lemma

B.2
(2)
, we conclude that
P^{+}
is a behavioural biconformance.

(6)

Immediate from part
(3)
.

(7)

By parts
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
we know that
1_{C}
,
P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}\cdot P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
, and
(P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow})^{\circ}
are behavioural biconformances on
(C,c)
. Since
P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
is the greatest behavioural biconformance, it follows that

1_{C}\sqsubseteq P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow},\qquad P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}\cdot P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}\sqsubseteq P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow},\qquad\text{and}\qquad(P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow})^{\circ}\sqsubseteq P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow},

which proves that
P_{c}^{\leftrightarrow}
is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive.∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.26

Remark B.4.

Recall that a
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
satisfies

f^{\star}P\cdot f^{\star}Q\sqsubseteq f^{\star}(P\cdot Q)\qquad\text{for all $f\colon X\to Y$ in $\mathbb{B}$}.

Using the adjunction
f_{\star}\dashv f^{\star}
, one readily verifies that this implies a corresponding property of
f_{\star}
:

f_{\star}(P\cdot Q)\sqsubseteq f_{\star}P\cdot f_{\star}Q\qquad\text{for all $f\colon X\to Y$ in $\mathbb{B}$}.

We now prove the two statements of
Lemma

5.26
.

(1)

This follows from the computation

a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(P\cdot Q)=a_{\star}(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma Q)\sqsubseteq a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P\cdot a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma Q\sqsubseteq P\cdot Q.

The first step uses that
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is monoidal, the second one that
p
is
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration, and the last one that
P
and
Q
are congruences.

(2)

This follows from the computation

\displaystyle a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P^{+}

\displaystyle=a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}P^{n})

def.
(-)^{+}

\displaystyle=a_{\star}\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P^{n}

\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
pres. dir. joins

\displaystyle=a_{\star}\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)^{n}

\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
monoidal

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}a_{\star}(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)^{n}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}(a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)^{n}

p
is a
\mathbf{Qtl}
-fibration

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}P^{n}

P
congruence

\displaystyle=P^{+}

def.
(-)^{+}

For the second step, note that the join
\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}P^{n}
is indeed directed: Since
P
is reflexive by assumption,

P=P\cdot 1\sqsubseteq P\cdot P=P\cdot P\cdot 1\sqsubseteq P\cdot P\cdot P=\cdots,

hence the objects
P^{n}
(
n\geq 1
) form an ascending chain in
{\mathbb{E}}_{A}
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.28

To prepare the proof we need a simple lemma:

Lemma B.5.

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
be an endofunctor with a
\circ
-monoidal lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to{\mathbb{E}}
along the
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration
p\colon{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{B}
. If
P
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence on a
\Sigma
-algebra
(A,a)
, then so is
P^{\circ}
.

Proof.

This follows from the computation

a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P^{\circ}\sqsubseteq a_{\star}(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)^{\circ}\sqsubseteq(a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma P)^{\circ}\sqsubseteq P^{\circ}.

The first step uses that
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal, the second one that
p
is an
\mathbf{InQtl}
-fibration, and third one that
P
is a congruence.
∎

We now prove the three statements of
Lemma

5.28
.

(1)

Suppose that
P
is reflexive. Then

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is reflexive because

1\sqsubseteq P\sqsubseteq P\sqcup(a_{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}})\cdot P=\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}};

the first step uses that
P
is reflexive and the third one holds by definition of

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

. To prove that

\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}

is a congruence on
(A,a)

Lemma

(2)
. To prove (

is a pre-fixed point, i.e.,

or equivalently

(B.5)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}^{\circ})^{+}

(B.6)

\displaystyle(a_{\star}\bar{\Sigma}(\mathchoice{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\textstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}{\scalebox{.71}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\displaystyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\textstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptstyle P$}}{\scalebox{1.4}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle P$}}}$}}^{\circ})^{+})\cdot P

The inequality (

Proof of
Lemma

7.2

For the proof of compatibility of
\mathcal{L}
, we need a continuity property of the Wasserstein distance. Recall that
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X)
carries a DCPO structure given by
\varphi\leq\varphi^{\prime}
iff
\varphi(x)\leq\varphi^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in X
.

Lemma B.7.

Let
(X,d)
be a fuzzy relation and let
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d=\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(d_{\bot})
be the induced Wasserstein metric on
{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X)
. Then for every directed family
\varphi_{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X)
,
k\in K
, and every
\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}(\{\bot\}+X)
,

\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\bigvee_{k\in K}\varphi_{k},\psi)=\bigvee_{k\in K}\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{k},\psi)

Proof.

Let
\varphi=\bigvee_{k\in K}\varphi_{k}
, and put
\varphi=p\cdot\bot+\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}\cdot x_{i}
and
\psi=q\cdot\bot+\sum_{j\in J}q_{j}\cdot y_{j}
. Recall that
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\psi)
is the optimal value of the transportation problem from
\varphi
to
\psi
, that is, of the linear program
\mathsf{TP}(\varphi,\psi)
shown below:

\begin{array}[]{ll@{}ll}\text{minimize}&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j}&\,d_{\bot}(x_{i},y_{j})\cdot t_{i,j}&\\
\text{subject to}&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}&\,t_{i,j}=p_{i}&(i\in I)\\
&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}&\,t_{i,j}=q_{j}&(j\in J)\\
&&\,t_{i,j}\geq 0&(i\in I,\,j\in J)\end{array}

Its dual linear program
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi,\psi)
is given by

\begin{array}[]{ll@{}ll}\text{maximize}&\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i}&a_{i}\cdot p_{i}+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j}b_{j}\cdot q_{j}&\\
\text{subject to}&&a_{i}+b_{j}\leq d_{\bot}(x_{i},y_{j})&(i\in I,\,j\in J)\\
&&a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}&(i\in I,\,j\in J)\\
&&(a_{i})_{i\in I},(b_{j})_{j\in J}\text{ bounded}&\end{array}

Both linear programs
\mathsf{TP}(\varphi,\psi)
and
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi,\psi)
have an optimal solution, and their optimal values coincide
(
Kortanek and Yamasaki 1995
, Thm. 2.1, Thm. 2.2)
. Take an optimal solution
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(b_{j})_{j\in J}
of
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi,\psi)
. Since the solution is bounded, there exists
a>0
such that
|a_{i}|\leq a
for all
i
. For every
\varphi^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}^{\prime}\cdot x_{i}
, we note that
(a_{i})
,
(b_{j})
is also a (not necessarily optimal) solution of
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi^{\prime},\psi)
, and the difference between the values of
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi,\psi)
and
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi^{\prime},\psi)
under these solutions is bounded:

(B.13)

|\sum_{i}a_{i}\cdot p_{i}-\sum_{i}a_{i}\cdot p_{i}^{\prime}|\leq a\cdot\sum_{i}|p_{i}-p_{i}^{\prime}|.

To prove the statement of the lemma, suppose towards a contradiction that

C:=\bigvee_{k\in K}\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{k},\psi)<\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\psi).

Since the join
\varphi=\bigvee_{k}\varphi_{k}
is directed, there exists
k\in K
such that

\sum_{i}|p_{i}-p_{i}^{\prime}|<(\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\psi)-C)/a,\qquad\text{where}\qquad\varphi_{k}=\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\prime}\cdot x_{i}.

By (
B.13
) this implies

|\sum_{i}a_{i}\cdot p_{i}-\sum_{i}a_{i}\cdot p_{i}^{\prime}|<\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi,\psi)-C.

In other words,
(a_{i})
,
(b_{j})
is a solution of
\mathsf{TP}_{\mathsf{d}}(\varphi_{k},\varphi)
whose value is greater than
C
. By duality, this means that the optimal solution of
\mathsf{TP}(\varphi_{k},\psi)
has a value greater than
C
, that is,
\mathrlap{\hskip 3.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.5}[1]{\phantom{$d$}}}}d(\varphi_{k},\psi)>C
, a contradiction.
∎

We split the statement of
Lemma

7.2
into four sublemmas.

Lemma B.8.

The lifting situation
\mathcal{L}=(p,\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma,\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B)
is compatible with
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{i}\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
(
i\in I
) be a
\leq
-directed family, let
g\colon\Lambda\to B(\Lambda,\Lambda)
, and let
d,e\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\to[0,1]
be fuzzy relations. We need to prove

(B.14)

(\bigvee_{i\in I}f_{i},g)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e)=\bigsqcap_{i\in I}\,(f_{i},g)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e).

To follows from the computation below for all
s,t\in\Lambda
:

\displaystyle((\bigvee_{i\in I}f_{i},g)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e))(s,t)

\displaystyle=\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e)(\bigvee_{i}f_{i}(s),g(t))

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{i}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e)(f_{i}(s),g(t))

by
Lemma

B.7

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{i}((f_{i},g)^{\star}\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B(d,e))(s,t).\qed

Lemma B.9.

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
is
\circ
-monoidal.

Proof.

We need to prove
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(d\cdot e)=\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma e
for all fuzzy relations
d,e\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
. It suffice to show
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(d\cdot e)(s,t)=(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma e)(s,t)
for elements
s,t\in\Sigma X
of the same shape, for otherwise both sides are
1
and the equality holds trivially. For all
n
-ary
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
and
x_{i},y_{i}\in X
(
i=1,\ldots,n
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma(d\cdot e)({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d\cdot e)(x_{i},y_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{z}\{d(x_{i},z)+e(z,y_{i})\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d(x_{i},z_{i})+e(z_{i},y_{i}))\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x_{i},z_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}e(z_{i},y_{i})\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})}\{\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}))+\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma e({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))\}

\displaystyle=(\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma e)({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})).\qed

Lemma B.10.

The map
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma\colon\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},X}\to\mathbf{FRel}_{{\mathbf{jne}},\Sigma X}
preserves directed joins.

Proof.

Let
d_{i}\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
be a
\sqsubseteq
-directed family of fuzzy relations with join
d
. We need to prove that

\Sigma d=\bigsqcup_{i}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d_{i}
. It suffices to show
\Sigma d(s,t)=(\bigsqcup_{i}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d_{i})(s,t)
for term
s,t\in\Sigma X
of the same shape, for otherwise both sides are
1
. For all
n
-ary
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\Sigma
and
x_{i},y_{i}\in X
(
i=1,\ldots,n
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}d(x_{k},y_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigwedge_{i}d_{i}(x_{k},y_{k})

\displaystyle=\bigwedge_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{n}d_{i}(x_{k},y_{k})

\displaystyle=(\bigsqcup_{i}\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma d)({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}))

The third step uses that the meet is directed and the sum is finite.
∎

Lemma B.11.

The lifting
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
is laxly monoidal.

Proof.

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B
is composed of
(-)_{\bot}
,
\mathrlap{\hskip 2.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$H$}}}}H
, and
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}
, so it suffices to show that all three lifted functors are laxly monoidal.

(1)

For
(-)_{\bot}
, we need to show for
d,e\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\{\bot\}+X
that

(d\cdot e)_{\bot}(x,x^{\prime})\leq(d_{\bot}\cdot e_{\bot})(x,x^{\prime}).

For
x=\bot
this holds because the left-hand side is
0
. For
x^{\prime}=\bot
it holds because either both sides are
0
or the right-hand side is
1
. For
x,x^{\prime}\in X
it also holds because both sides are equal to
(d\cdot e)(x,y)
.

(2)

For
H
, we need to prove for
d,d^{\prime}\colon X\to[0,1]
,
e,e^{\prime}\colon Y\times Y\to[0,1]
and
f,g\in Y^{X}
that

(B.15)

(e\cdot e^{\prime})^{d\cdot d^{\prime}}(f,g)\leq(e^{d}\cdot e^{\prime d^{\prime}})(f,g).

For all
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in X
and
h\in Y^{X}
we have

\displaystyle(e\cdot e^{\prime})(f(x),g(x^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq e(f(x),h(x^{\prime}))+e^{\prime}(h(x^{\prime}),g(x^{\prime\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d(x,x^{\prime})+e^{d}(f,h)+d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})+e^{\prime d^{\prime}}(h,g)

\displaystyle=d(x,x^{\prime})+d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})+e^{d}(f,h)+e^{\prime d^{\prime}}(h,g).

Taking the infimum over all
h\in Y^{X}
and
x^{\prime}\in X
, thus proves

(e\cdot e^{\prime})(f(x),g(x^{\prime\prime}))\leq(d\cdot d^{\prime})(x,x^{\prime\prime})+(e^{d}\cdot e^{\prime d^{\prime}})(f,g).

Since this inequality holds for all
x,x^{\prime\prime}\in X
, we conclude (
B.15
).

(3)

For
{\mathcal{D}}
, let
d,e\colon X\times X\to[0,1]
. For
\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}X
we need to prove that

\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}(d\cdot e)(\varphi,\psi)\leq(\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}d\cdot\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{${\mathcal{D}}$}}}}{\mathcal{D}}e)(\varphi,\psi).

For this it suffices to prove that for every
\xi\in{\mathcal{D}}X
, every transportation plan from
\varphi
to
\xi
along
d
of cost
C
, and every transportation plan from
\xi
to
\psi
along
e
of cost
C^{\prime}
, there is a transportation plan from
\varphi
to
\psi
along
d\cdot e
of cost at most
C+C^{\prime}
. But this is clear: Just compose the two transportation plans in the obvious way.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

7.4

(1)

d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is adequate: apply its definition to the trivial context
C=[\cdot]
.

(2)

d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence: Let
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
in
\Sigma
and let
C[\cdot]
be a context of input type, and let
t_{i},s_{i}\in\Lambda
for
i=\ldots,n
. We define

C_{i}[\cdot]:=C[f(s_{1},\cdots,s_{i-1},\cdot\,,t_{i+1},\cdots,t_{n})]\qquad(i=1,\ldots,n).

Note that

C_{1}[t_{1}]=C[f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})],\quad C_{n}[s_{n}]=C[f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})],\quad C_{i}[s_{i}]=C_{i+1}[t_{i+1}]\;\;(i=1,\ldots,n-1).

It follows that

\displaystyle~|\gamma(C[{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})])(\bot)-\gamma(C[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}](\bot))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\,\gamma(C_{i}[t_{i}])(\bot)-\gamma(C_{i}[s_{i}])(\bot)\,\big)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\gamma(C_{i}[t_{i}])(\bot)-\gamma(C_{i+1}[s_{i}])(\bot)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t_{i},s_{i}).

Since the above inequality holds for all contexts
C[\cdot]
, we conclude

d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}),{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t_{i},s_{i}),

proving that
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
is
\bar{\Sigma}
-congruence.

(3)

Every adequate
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence
d
on
(\Lambda,\iota)
satisfies
d\sqsubseteq d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
: Let
C[\cdot]
be a context and
t,s\in\Lambda
. Let us first observe that
d(C[t],C[s])\leq d(t,s)
. To see this, we proceed by structural induction on the context
C[\cdot]
. For the empty context
C=[\cdot]
the statement is trivial. Thus suppose that
C={\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{i-1},C^{\prime}[\cdot],t_{i+1},\cdots,t_{n})
for some
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
in
\Sigma
and some context
C^{\prime}[\cdot]
. Then

d(C[t],C[s])\leq d(C^{\prime}[t],C^{\prime}[s])+\sum_{j\neq i}d(t_{j},t_{j})\leq d(t,s)

where the first uses that
d
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence and the second one follows by induction; note that
d(t_{j},t_{j})=0
because
d
is a
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma
-congruence and thus a pseudometric.

By adequacy of
d
is now follows that

|\gamma(C[t])(\bot)-\gamma(C[s])(\bot)|\leq d(C[t],C[s])\leq d(t,s),

hence
d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}(t,s)\leq d(t,s)
for all
t,s
, which proves
d\sqsubseteq d_{\mathrm{ctx}}^{\mathbf{pBCKI}}
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.9

For ease of notation, we denote by
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R
the derived relations on

(\Lambda,R)^{(\Lambda,R)},\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(\{\bot\}+(\Lambda,R)^{(\Lambda,R)}),\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),(\Lambda,R)),\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$B$}}}}B((\Lambda,R),(\Lambda,R))),\quad\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma^{\star}$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,R),

etc. Non-expansivity of
\varrho
means that if
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
in
\Sigma
and
(t_{i},\varphi_{i},\Phi_{i}),(t_{i}^{\prime},\varphi_{i}^{\prime},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\in\Lambda\times{B}(\Lambda,\Lambda)\times{B}(\Lambda,{B}(\Lambda,\Lambda))
,

\displaystyle R(t_{i},t_{i})\text{ and }\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}^{\prime})\text{ and }\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(\Phi_{i},\Phi_{i}^{\prime})\quad\text{for all $i=1,\dots,n$}

\displaystyle\implies~

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},\varphi_{1},\Phi_{1}),\ldots,(t_{n},\varphi_{n},\Phi_{n}))),\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1}^{\prime},\varphi_{1}^{\prime},\Phi_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(t_{n}^{\prime},\varphi_{n}^{\prime},\Phi_{n}^{\prime})))).

For all operations
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}
except
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}
this follows from
Lemma

7.3
restricted to relations (
\{0,1\}
-valued fuzzy relations).
For
{\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}\in\{S,S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\}
the map
\varrho
is defined as

\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n},-,-)))=1\cdot(t\mapsto T)

where
T=T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\in\Sigma^{\star}\Lambda
is a term in the variables
t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\Lambda
. Now let
t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and suppose that
R(t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. By definition of the relation
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R
of the term algebra
\mathrlap{\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\scalebox{0.9}[1]{\phantom{$\Sigma$}}}}\Sigma^{\star}(\Lambda,R)
, we have for all
t,t^{\prime}\in\Lambda
that

R(t,t^{\prime})\implies\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(T[t,t_{1},\dots,t_{n}],T[t^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}]).

This means that the functions
t\mapsto T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
and
t\mapsto T[t,t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}]
are related in the hom-set relation from
Example

5.15
(4)
. Therefore,
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(1\cdot(t\mapsto T[t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]),1\cdot(t\mapsto T[t,t_{1}^{\prime}\ldots,t_{n}^{\prime}]))
and so
\mathrlap{\hskip 1.65pt\overline{\scalebox{0.6}[1]{\phantom{$R$}}}}R(\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n},-,-)),\varrho({\operatorname{\mathsf{f}}}((t_{1}^{\prime},-,-),\ldots,(t_{n}^{\prime},-,-))))
, as required.
